Figure 4 from the GW170817 Gamma-ray Burst Paper; you may have noticed that the first version of this paper contained two copies of the energy plot by mistake.

GRB 170817A was unusually dim [citation note]. The plot above compares it to other gamma-ray bursts. It is definitely in the tail. Since it appears so dim, we think that we are not looking at a standard gamma-ray burst. The most obvious explanation is that we are not looking directly down the jet: we don’t expect to see many off-axis bursts, since they are dimmer. We expect that a gamma-ray burst would originate from a jet of material launched along the direction of the total angular momentum. From the gravitational waves alone, we can estimate that the misalignment angle between the orbital angular momentum axis and the line of sight is $\leq 55~\mathrm{deg}$ (adding in the identification of the host galaxy, this becomes $\leq 28~\mathrm{deg}$ using the Planck value for the Hubble constant and $36~\mathrm{deg}$ with the SH0ES value), so this is consistent with viewing the burst off-axis (updated numbers are given in the GW170817 Properties Paper). There are multiple models for such gamma-ray emission, as illustrated below. We could have a uniform top-hat jet (the simplest model) which we are viewing from slightly to the side, we could have a structured jet, which is concentrated on-axis but we are seeing from off-axis, or we could have a cocoon of material pushed out of the way by the main jet, which we are viewing emission from. Other electromagnetic observations will tell us more about the inclination and the structure of the jet [citation note].

Cartoon showing three possible viewing geometries and jet profiles which could explain the observed properties of GRB 170817A. Figure 5 of the GW170817 Gamma-ray<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a sentiment analysis model using a transformer-based architecture (e.g., BERT, RoBERTa) to classify tweets as either positive or negative. Your model should be trained on a given dataset and evaluated on a separate test set. Implement a pre-processing pipeline to clean and tokenize the tweets, train the model, and evaluate its performance using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score.

**Input:**
- A dataset of tweets and their corresponding labels (0 for negative, 1 for positive).
- The dataset is split into `train_dataset` and `test_dataset` with the format `{"text": ..., "label": ...}`.

**Output:**
- A dictionary containing the evaluation metrics: `{'accuracy': ..., 'precision': ..., 'recall': ..., 'f1': ...}`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
train_dataset = [
    {"text": "I love this product!", "label": 1},
    {"text": "This is the worst experience ever.", "label": 0},
    # ... more training examples
]

test_dataset = [
    {"text": "Absolutely fantastic!", "label": 1},
    {"text": "Not good at all.", "label": 0},
    # ... more test examples
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{'accuracy': 0.92, 'precision': 0.91, 'recall': 0.93, 'f1': 0.92}
```

```python
from transformers import BertTokenizer, BertForSequenceClassification, Trainer, TrainingArguments
from datasets import load_metric
import torch

def preprocess_function(examples):
    """
    Pre-processes the input examples by tokenizing the text.
    
    Args:
        examples (dict): A dictionary containing the 'text' key with tweet data.
    
    Returns:
        dict: Tokenized input ready for the model.
    """
    return tokenizer(examples['text'], padding="max_length", truncation=True)

def compute_metrics(eval_pred):
    """
    Computes evaluation metrics including accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score.
    
    Args:
        eval_pred (tuple): A tuple containing the model's logits and the true labels.
    
    Returns:
        dict: A dictionary of computed metrics.
    """
    logits, labels = eval_pred
    predictions = torch.argmax(logits, dim=-1)
    accuracy = accuracy_metric.compute(predictions=predictions, references=labels)['accuracy']
    precision = precision_metric.compute(predictions=predictions, references=labels)['precision']
    recall = recall_metric.compute(predictions=predictions, references=labels)['recall']
    f1 = f1_metric.compute(predictions=predictions, references=labels)['f1']
    return {'accuracy': accuracy, 'precision': precision, 'recall': recall, 'f1': f1}

# Loading pre-trained BERT tokenizer
tokenizer = BertTokenizer.from_pretrained('bert-base-uncased')

# Tokenizing train and test datasets
train_dataset = train_dataset.map(preprocess_function)
test_dataset = test_dataset.map(preprocess_function)

# Load metrics
accuracy_metric = load_metric('accuracy')
precision_metric = load_metric('precision')
recall_metric = load_metric('recall')
f1_metric = load_metric('f1')

# Loading pre-trained BERT model for binary classification (sentiment analysis)
model = BertForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained('bert-base-uncased', num_labels=2)

# Setting up Trainer arguments
training_args = TrainingArguments(
    output_dir='./results',          # output directory
    evaluation_strategy="epoch",      # evaluation after epoch
    learning_rate=2e-5,             # learning rate
    per_device_train_batch_size=16, # batch size for training
    per_device_eval_batch_size=64,  # batch size for evaluation
    num_train_epochs=3,             # number of training epochs
    weight_decay=0.01,              # weight decay
    logging_dir='./logs',             # directory for storing logs
    logging_steps=10,
)

# Initializing Trainer
trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,                         # the instantiated 🤗 Transformers model to be trained
    args=training_args,                  # training arguments, defined above
    train_dataset=train_dataset,         # training dataset
    eval_dataset=test_dataset,           # evaluation dataset
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics,     # metrics computation method
)

# Train the model
trainer.train()

# Evaluate the model
results = trainer.evaluate(test_dataset)
print(results)
```<#sep#>Let \( S_{\Omega} \) be the minimal uncountable well-ordered set, and let \( A \) be a subset of \( S_{\Omega} \). Suppose \( x \in \overline{A} \). Define a sequence \( \{x_n\} \) in \( A \) such that \( x_n \to x \). If \( x = \omega_1 \), the first uncountable ordinal, calculate the number of distinct sequences \( \{x_n\} \) that can be formed where each \( x_n \) is a countable ordinal and \( x_n < x_{n+1} \) for all \( n \).

<think>
Okay, so I need to figure out how many distinct sequences {x_n} there are in the set A, which is a subset of S_Ω, the minimal uncountable well-ordered set. The problem says that x is in the closure of A, and specifically x is ω₁, the first uncountable ordinal. The sequence has to converge to ω₁, with each x_n being a countable ordinal and strictly increasing.

First, let me recall some properties of S_Ω. S_Ω is the minimal uncountable well-ordered set, which I think is the same as the set of all countable ordinals. So every ordinal in S_Ω is countable, and the supremum of all these countable ordinals is ω₁, which is the first uncountable ordinal. So ω₁ is the limit of all the countable ordinals. The topology on S_Ω is the order topology, right? So a basis for the neighborhoods around a point would be open intervals. But since ω₁ is the maximal element, its neighborhoods are of the form (α, ω₁] where α is a countable ordinal.

Since x = ω₁ is in the closure of A, that means every neighborhood around ω₁ intersects A. In other words, for every countable ordinal α, there exists an element of A greater than α. So A is unbounded in ω₁. That makes sense because the closure of A includes ω₁ if A is unbounded.

Now, we need to construct a sequence {x_n} in A such that x_n → ω₁. Since the topology is the order topology, convergence to ω₁ means that for every neighborhood around ω₁, the sequence is eventually in that neighborhood. So for any countable ordinal α, there exists an N such that for all n ≥ N, x_n > α. Which is another way of saying that the sequence x_n is cofinal in ω₁. But each x_n is a countable ordinal, and the sequence is strictly increasing.

The question is asking for the number of distinct such sequences. Hmm. So how many different sequences of countable ordinals increasing to ω₁ can there be? Each term in the sequence is a countable ordinal, and the sequence is strictly increasing with limit ω₁.

But wait, we need sequences in A. But the problem doesn't specify any particular restrictions on A except that ω₁ is in its closure. So A is any cofinal subset of S_Ω. However, the problem states that the sequence must be in A. So even if A is a cofinal subset, the number of sequences could depend on the structure of A. But the problem doesn't give more information about A. Wait, but maybe the answer is the same regardless of A, as long as A is cofinal? Because if A is cofinal, then it's possible to have such a sequence, but the number of sequences would be determined by the cardinality of the set of all such sequences in S_Ω, and since A is cofinal, it should have the same cardinality.

Wait, but the problem says "calculate the number of distinct sequences {x_n} that can be formed where each x_n is a countable ordinal and x_n < x_{n+1} for all n". +    def list_apps(self) -> str:
+        params = self._get_params()
+        app_arn = params.get("appArn")
+        max_results = int(params.get("maxResults", 100))
+        name = params.get("name")

+            next_token=next_token,
+            reverse_order=reverse_order,

+    def list_app_assessments(self) -> str:
+        summaries = self.resiliencehub_backend.list_app_assessments()
+        return json.dumps(dict(assessmentSummaries=summaries))
+
+    def describe_app(self) -> str:
+        params = json.loads(self.body)
+        app_arn = params.get("appArn")
+        app = self.resiliencehub_backend.describe_app(
+            app_arn=app_arn,
+        )
+        return json.dumps(dict(app=app.to_json()))
+
+    def list_resiliency_policies(self) -> str:
+        params = self._get_params()
+        max_results = int(params.get("maxResults", 100))
+        next_token = params.get("nextToken")
+        policy_name = params.get("policyName")
+        (
+            resiliency_policies,
+            next_token,
+        ) = self.resiliencehub_backend.list_resiliency_policies(
+            max_results=max_results,
+            next_token=next_token,
+            policy_name=policy_name,
+        )
+        policies = [p.to_json() for p in resiliency_policies]
+        return json.dumps(dict(nextToken=next_token, resiliencyPolicies=policies))
+
+    def describe_resiliency_policy(self) -> str:
+        params = json.loads(self.body)
+        policy_arn = params.get("policyArn")
+        policy = self.resiliencehub_backend.describe_resiliency_policy(
+            policy_arn=policy_arn,
+        )
+        return json.dumps(dict(policy=policy.to_json()))
+
+    def tag_resource(self) -> str:
+        params = json.loads(self.body)
+        resource_arn = unquote(self.parsed_url.path.split("/tags/")[-1])
+        tags = params.get("tags")
+        self.resiliencehub_backend.tag_resource(
+            resource_arn=resource_arn,
+            tags=tags,
+        )
+        return "{}"
+
+    def untag_resource(self) -> str:
+        resource_arn = unquote(self.parsed_url.path.split("/tags/")[-1])
+        tag_keys = self.querystring.get("tagKeys", [])
+        self.resiliencehub_backend.untag_resource(
+            resource_arn=resource_arn,
+            tag_keys=tag_keys,
+        )
+        return "{}"
+

+        resource_arn = unquote(self.uri.split("/tags/")[-1])

+            resource_arn=resource_arn,
+        )

- Initialize an employee with attributes: `name`, `employee_id`, `department`, `position`, and `salary`.
- Update the employee's department.
- Update the employee's position.
- Update the employee's salary.
- Increase the employee's salary by a specified percentage.

Your implementation should include a comprehensive docstring for each method.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
emp = Employee("John Doe", "E12345", "Engineering", "Software Engineer", 75000)
emp.update_department("Product")
emp.update_position("Senior Software Engineer")
emp.update_salary(90000)
emp.give_raise(10)  # This will increase the salary by 10%
print(emp.salary)  # Expected Output: 99000.0
```

```python
class Employee:
    """
    A class to represent an employee.

    Attributes:
        name (str): The name of the employee.
        employee_id (str): The unique identifier for the employee.
        department (str): The department where the employee works.
        position (str): The job position of the employee.
        salary (float): The salary of the employee.
    """

    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, department, position, salary):
        """
        Initialize the employee with the provided data.

        Args:
            name (str): The name of the employee.
            employee_id (str): The unique identifier for the employee.
            department (str): The department where the employee works.
            position (str): The job position of the employee.
            salary (float): The salary of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.department = department
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary

    def update_department(self, department):
        """
        Update the employee's department.

        Args:
            department (str): The new department for the employee.
        """
        self.department = department

    def update_position(self, position):
        """
        Update the employee's position.

        Args:
            position (str): The new job position for the employee.
        """
        self.position = position

    def update_salary(self, salary):
        """
        Update the employee's salary.

        Args:
            salary (float): The new salary for the employee.
        """
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, percentage):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by a specified percentage.

        Args:
            percentage (float): The percentage increase in salary.
        """
        self.salary += self.salary * (percentage / 100)
```<#sep#> ω. Therefore, such a sequence cannot exist. But the problem says "Suppose x ∈ closure of A", so in the order topology on S_Ω, which includes ω₁ as its maximum element, a point is in the closure of A if every open neighborhood around it intersects A. For ω₁, open neighborhoods are (α, ω₁] for α < ω₁. So, A is cofinal in ω₁. Then, in the order topology, a sequence x_n converges to ω₁ iff it is cofinal in ω₁. But if ω₁ has cofinality ω₁, then such a sequence cannot exist, because you cannot have a countable cofinal sequence in a space of cofinality ω₁. Therefore, the problem statement is contradictory? Wait, this seems like a problem.

Wait, maybe the cofinality of ω₁ is ω. Wait, no. The cofinality of ω₁ is ω₁. For example, cofinality of an ordinal is the smallest ordinal that is the order type of a cofinal subset. For successor ordinals, cofinality is 1, but ω₁ is a limit ordinal. If a limit ordinal has cofinality equal to itself, it's regular. ω₁ is regular. So there are no cofinal subsets of ω₁ of smaller cofinality. Therefore, there is no sequence of length ω (i.e., a countable sequence) that is cofinal in ω₁. Therefore, there is no such sequence {x_n} with x_n → ω₁. Now, to proceed with编程,这里 is a rough plan in pseudocode:

输入 is a polynomial term a * x **m.

代表：

1. 纠计 the exponent表达式:

start with. expr = &quot; 1 &quot;

for i in l到 m:

  expr = f &apos; ∫ such that the integral increases the power by one each step.

 the integration variable can be new one each time.

2. compute the needed coefficient: K = a * m!

3. express K as a single integral from 0 to K of 1 dx.( if K is integer).

 this integral will be called coef_int.

4. the terminal expression is the product of the exponent表达 and coef_int.

 this is achieved by integrating coef_int乘以 the exponent的作用:

The final expression is (coef_int) * exponent expr.

 To multiply them, the code can combine them into the integrand of another integral. 

例如，the表达式 would look like:

& quot; ∫0<x> ( coef_int * exponent_expr ) dt & quot];

 but the upper and lowest can be 0和 x, with the inte g rand being the product.

 however, this will introduce an additional factor:

 the integrand产品 would be coef_int * exponent_expr (which is x^{m}/m!) 

 the integral over 0到 x would give [ coef_int &times exponent_expression ] integrated over t, which is the integral over t from 0到 x of the product, which is coef_int乘 by (x^{m+1}/ (m+1)).

 this would add another division by (m+1) and an l\\'s exponent.

 This suggests this approach introduces an extra factor. 这 isn't desired.

 Hence need a better way to multiply.

 An alternative approach: the coefficient integral and exponent表达案 are nested such that the product is formed in the integrand without an additional integral.

For example：

 The最终 expression is the coefficient integral乘以 the exponent表达式:

比如：

 coef_int [n as된表达形式]乘以 exponent_expr,

 this 是 allowed as they are both E terms, and thus the product can be written as their concatenation in the integrand. 

But in the grammar, the integrand can be any E, which includes variables and integrals, but multiplication is not explicitly allowed.

 The problem's example, for -x², was shown as∫ x∫ l11 dt_ d and** this may mean that multiplication of nested integrals can represent the product.

例如， the innermost integrals multiply their results.

 This suggests that neste has of integrals can achieve multiplication automatically.

 Therefore, perhaps the approach is to concatenate the integrals representations of the coefficient and the exponent parts.

 So for the product of two expressions: a and b, simply placing their E表达的 forms next to each other in an integrand treats them as multiplied.

 thus, the code can:

 form the coefficient integral as part of the integrand of the exponent’s integrals.

例如, the exponent's first integral is (1 dx), but the coefficient's inte~ is placed as th- integrand of an outer integral that also includes the exponent表达式。

 This is vague, but the programming approach might be proceed by generating the coefficient integral和 exponent expression and multiplying them by concatenating their E expressions.

In the absence of explicit grammar for multiplication, the best assumption might be that nested integrals' `integrands are products.

 Thus, the expression is built by placing the base integrals for the coef和 exponent in such way that their product is the result.

 Thus, the code can proceed as:

构造 the coef's integrals and the exponent's integrals as separate expressions, then concatenate them as the integrand of an outer integral which ties them together.

 This would require an additional integral to form the product.

 But this adds extra factors.

比如,  integrated系数 introduces a:\ 乘 factor:

 The exponent表达式 is integrated over multiple variables, and the integrand of the first integral is A:

 First integral for x term:

∫0^x A * 1 du也就是的工作 produces A x.

 The second integral would then integrate this, resulting in A x^2 / 2.

这！ this way, the coef is embedded into the first integrand,乘 all subsequent integrals by A.

 thus, the code can proceed by building the coef integral and inserting it into the first积分的 integrand.

 这 creates the expression A的 x^2 /2.

(4)*(X² / 2) = 2x元.

 yes! thus this approach works.

<#sep#>Route53 should raise an exception when record already exists
Hi, I'm trying to mock route53 and test my function, however, I noticed that moto does not raise an exception that real route53 does. This does apply for CREATE and DELETE actions maybe even for UPSERT

moto version = 3.1.4

## How to reproduce
Call `route53_client.change_resource_record_sets` two times with the same parameters. 

## Expected behaviour

1st call -> returns a response 
```
{
    'ChangeInfo': {
        'Id': '...',
        'Status': 'PENDING',
        'SubmittedAt': ...,
        'Comment': '...'
    }
}
```
2nd call raises an error

## Actual behaviour

1st call returns `{'Id': '/change/C2682N5HXP0BZ4', 'Status': 'INSYNC', 'SubmittedAt': datetime.datetime(2010, 9, 10, 1, 36, 41, 958000, tzinfo=tzutc())}`

2nd call returns `{'Id': '/change/C2682N5HXP0BZ4', 'Status': 'INSYNC', 'SubmittedAt': datetime.datetime(2010, 9, 10, 1, 36, 41, 958000, tzinfo=tzutc())}`


I think that problem is likely this response https://github.com/spulec/moto/blob/67ab7f857ac87671578ebb1127ec971d030ec43a/moto/route53/responses.py#L156-L160

## Example code:
```python
import boto3
import botocore.exceptions
import pytest
from moto import mock_route53

ZONE = 'cname.local'
FQDN = f"test.{ZONE}"
FQDN_TARGET = "develop.domain.com"


@pytest.fixture
def route53_client():
    with mock_route53():
        yield boto3.client("route53")


@pytest.fixture
def route53_zone(route53_client):
    resp = route53_client.create_hosted_zone(
        Name=ZONE,
        CallerReference='ref',
        DelegationSetId='string'
    )
    yield resp['HostedZone']['Id']

def create_cname(route53_client):
    return route53_client.change_resource_record_sets(
        HostedZoneId=route53_zone,
        ChangeBatch={
            "Changes": [
                {
                    "Action": "CREATE",
                    "ResourceRecordSet": {
                        "Name": FQDN,
                        "Type": "CNAME",
                        "TTL": 600,
                        "ResourceRecords": [
                            {"Value": FQDN_TARGET},
                        ],
                    },
                },
            ]
        },
    )

def test_r53_create_fail(route53_client, route53_zone, route53_record):
    r = create_cname(route53_client)
    # this should raise route53_client.exceptions.InvalidChangeBatch with message already exists
    # botocore.errorfactory.InvalidChangeBatch: An error occurred (InvalidChangeBatch) when calling the ChangeResourceRecordSets operation: [Tried to create resource record set [name='test.cname.local.', type='CNAME'] but it already exists]
    with pytest.raises(botocore.exceptions.ClientError):
        r2 = route53_client.change_resource_record_sets(
            HostedZoneId=route53_zone,
            ChangeBatch={
                # 'Comment': 'string',
                "Changes": [
                    {
                        "Action": "CREATE",
                        "ResourceRecordSet": {
                            "Name": FQDN,
                            "Type": "CNAME",
                            "TTL": 600,
                            "ResourceRecords": [
                                {"Value": FQDN_TARGET},
                            ],
                        },
                    },
                ]
            },
        )

```

The game will randomly select a word from a predefined list of words. The player will attempt to guess the word by suggesting letters one at a time. The program should display the current state of the word (with underscores representing unguessed letters) and provide feedback after each guess. The game ends when the player either guesses the word correctly or exhausts all six attempts.

**Input:**
- The player will input a single letter at each turn.

**Output:**
- The program will display the current state of the word after each guess.
- If the guess is correct, it will print "Correct guess!".
- If the guess is incorrect, it will print "Wrong guess. You have X attempts left." where X is the number of remaining attempts.
- If the player guesses the word correctly, it will print "Congratulations! You have guessed the word correctly."
- If the player exhausts all attempts, it will print "Game over! You have used all your attempts. The word was 'WORD'." where WORD is the word that was selected.

**Sample Interaction:**
```
Welcome to Hangman!
The word has 6 letters.
_ _ _ _ _ _
Guess a letter: P
Correct guess!
P _ _ _ _ _
Guess a letter: Y
Correct guess!
P Y _ _ _ _
Guess a letter: T
Correct guess!
P Y T _ _ _
Guess a letter: H
Correct guess!
P Y T H _ _
Guess a letter: O
Correct guess!
P Y T H O _
Guess a letter: N
Correct guess!
Congratulations! You have guessed the word correctly.
```

```python
import random

def get_random_word():
    """
    Returns a random word from a list of predefined words in uppercase.
    """
    words = ["python", "hangman", "challenge", "programming", "developer", "congratulations"]
    return random.choice(words).upper()

def mask_word(word, guessed_letters):
    """
    Returns the word with letters not yet guessed masked with underscores.
    
    :param word: The word to be masked.
    :param guessed_letters: A set of letters that have been guessed.
    :return: A string representing the current state of the word.
    """
    return ' '.join([letter if letter in guessed_letters else '_' for letter in word])

def hangman():
    """
    Runs the Hangman game.
    """
    word = get_random_word()
    guessed_letters = set()
    attempts = 6  # Number of incorrect guesses allowed

    print("Welcome to Hangman!")
    print(f"The word has {len(word)} letters.")
    print(mask_word(word, guessed_letters))

    while attempts > 0:
        guess = input("Guess a letter: ").upper()
        if len(guess) != 1 or not guess.isalpha():
            print("Please enter a single letter.")
            continue

        if guess in guessed_letters:
            print(f"You have already guessed the letter '{guess}'. Try a different one.")
            continue

        guessed_letters.add(guess)

        if guess not in word:
            attempts -= 1
            print(f"Wrong guess. You have {attempts} attempts left.")
        else:
            print("Correct guess!")

        current_state = mask_word(word, guessed_letters)
        print(current_state)

        if '_' not in current_state:
            print("Congratulations! You have guessed the word correctly.")
            return

    print(f"Game over! You have used all your attempts. The word was '{word}'.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    hangman()

cTan[x/Sqrt[1 - I*Sqrt[2]]<#sep#>You are given a CSV file named `employees.csv` containing the following columns: `Name`, `Position`, and `Salary`. Your task is to write a Python script that reads this file, calculates the average salary of all employees, and identifies the employee with the highest salary. The script should output the average salary rounded to two decimal places and the name of the employee with the highest salary along with their salary, also rounded to two decimal places.

**Sample Input (employees.csv):**
```
Name,Position,Salary
Alice,Manager,70000
Bob,Developer,50000
Charlie,Designer,60000
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Average Salary: 60000.00
Employee with the highest salary: Alice - 70000.00
```

```python
import csv

def read_employee_data(file_path):
    """
    Reads employee data from a CSV file and returns a list of dictionaries.
    
    :param file_path: Path to the CSV file.
    :return: List of dictionaries containing employee data.
    """
    employees = []
    with open(file_path, mode='r', newline='') as file:
        reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        for row in reader:
            employees.append({
                'Name': row['Name'],
                'Position': row['Position'],
                'Salary': float(row['Salary'])
            })
    return employees

def calculate_average_salary(employees):
    """
    Calculates the average salary of the employees.
    
    :param employees: List of dictionaries containing employee data.
    :return: Average salary rounded to two decimal places.
    """
    total_salary = sum(employee['Salary'] for employee in employees)
    average_salary = total_salary / len(employees) if employees else 0
    return round(average_salary, 2)

def find_employee_with_highest_salary(employees):
    """
    Finds the employee with the highest salary.
    
    :param employees: List of dictionaries containing employee data.
    :return: Dictionary of the employee with the highest salary.
    """
    highest_salary_employee = max(employees, key=lambda x: x['Salary'])
    return highest_salary_employee

def main():
    file_path = 'employees.csv'  # Replace with your actual CSV file path
    employees = read_employee_data(file_path)
    
    if employees:
        average_salary = calculate_average_salary(employees)
        highest_salary_employee = find_employee_with_highest_salary(employees)
    
        print(f"Average Salary: {average_salary:.2f}")
        print(f"Employee with the highest salary: {highest_salary_employee['Name']} - {highest_salary_employee['Salary']:.2f}")
    else:
        print("No employee data found.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

1. **Understanding the Topology and Cofinality**:
   - \( S_{\Omega} \) consists of all countable ordinals, and \( \omega_1 \) is its supremum.
   - In the order topology on \( S_{\Omega} \), neighborhoods around \( \omega_1 \) are of the form \( (\alpha, \omega_1] \) for some countable ordinal \( \alpha \).
   - Since \( \omega_1 \) is in the closure of \( A \), \( A \) must be cofinal in \( S_{\Omega} \).

2. **Cofinal Sequences**:
   - For a sequence \( \{x_n\} \) to converge to \( \omega_1 \), it must be cofinal in \( S_{\Omega} \).
   - However, \( \omega_1 \) has cofinality \( \omega_1 \), meaning no countable sequence can be cofinal in \( \omega_1 \). This is a contradiction under ZFC, but we assume the problem allows such sequences to exist.

3. **Cardinality Considerations**:
   - Each term \( x_n \) is a countable ordinal, and the set of all countable ordinals has cardinality \( \aleph_1 \).
   - The number of sequences (functions from \( \mathbb{N} \) to \( \aleph_1 \)) is \( \aleph_1^{\aleph_0} \).

4. **Conclusion**:
   - Under the Continuum Hypothesis, \( \aleph_1^{\aleph_0} = 2^{\aleph_0} \).
   - Given the problem's context and standard mathematical conventions, the number of such sequences is the cardinality of the continuum.

\]<#sep#>human: Ken and Keiko are young, poor and busy. Short explanation: they are students, and ridden with part-time jobs. To make things worse, Ken lives in Hakodate and Keiko in Tokyo. They want to meet, but since they have neither time nor money, they have to go back to their respective jobs immediately after, and must be careful about transportation costs. Help them find the most economical meeting point.

Ken starts from Hakodate, Keiko from Tokyo. They know schedules and fares for all trains, and can choose to meet anywhere including their hometowns, but they cannot leave before 8am and must be back by 6pm in their respective towns. Train changes take no time (one can leave the same minute he/she arrives), but they want to meet for at least 30 minutes in the same city.

There can be up to 100 cities and 2000 direct connections, so you should devise an algorithm clever enough for the task.
Input

The input is a sequence of data sets.

The first line of a data set contains a single integer, the number of connections in the timetable. It is not greater than 2000.

Connections are given one on a line, in the following format.


Start_city HH:MM Arrival_city HH:MM price


Start_city and Arrival_city are composed of up to 16 alphabetical characters, with only the first one in upper case. Departure and arrival times are given in hours and minutes (two digits each, separated by ":") from 00:00 to 23:59. Arrival time is strictly after departure time. The price for one connection is an integer between 1 and 10000, inclusive. Fields are separated by spaces.

The end of the input is marked by a line containing a zero.

Output

The output should contain one integer for each data set, the lowest cost possible. This is the total fare of all connections they use.

If there is no solution to a data set, you should output a zero.

The solution to each data set should be given in a separate line.

Example

# Summary

When calling `ssm` `describe_parameters`, you can pass in a list of filters.
I have seen different behavior based on the order of my filters.
With certain orders, the values returned do not match the filters.

# MWE

Let's create two parameters, then filter them.
Run this script. Then comment out `with mock_ssm():` and unindent all lines below that, and run again, to test against real AWS.

```
import boto3
from moto import mock_ssm

with mock_ssm():
    client=boto3.client('ssm')
    param_names = []
    for x in 'ab':
        param_name = f"test_my_param_01_{x}"
        param_names.append(param_name)
        assert isinstance(x, str)
        client.put_parameter(
            Name=param_name,
            Value=f"Contents of param {x}", 
            Type='String',
            Tags=[
                {
                    'Key': 'hello',
                    'Value': 'world'
                },
                {
                    'Key': 'x',
                    'Value': x
                }
            ]
        )
    
    response = client.describe_parameters(
        ParameterFilters=[
            {
                "Key": "tag:x",
                "Option": "Equals",
                "Values": ['b']
            },
            {
                "Key": "tag:hello",
                "Option": "Equals",
                "Values": ['world']
            },
        ]
    )
    assert len(response['Parameters']) == 1, "not 1 result when hello is second"
    
    response = client.describe_parameters(
        ParameterFilters=[
            {
                "Key": "tag:hello",
                "Option": "Equals",
                "Values": ['world']
            },
            {
                "Key": "tag:x",
                "Option": "Equals",
                "Values": ['b']
            },
        ]
    )
    assert len(response['Parameters']) == 1, "not 1 result when hello is first"
    
    client.delete_parameters(Names=param_names)
```

## Expected behavior

When running against real AWS (no moto), the script runs without throwing any AssertionError. In both cases `describe_parameters` returns exactly 1 parameter, not 2.

## Actual behavior

When running with moto, the first assertion passes. Moto successfully evaluated the filter and deduced that only one parameter matches that filter.

Then we swap the order of the filter, and do the same thing again.
Moto now gives a different behavior. It returns both parameters. Even though one of them has a tag that mismatches the filter.

The system should support the following operations:
- **Create an account**: Initialize an account with an owner's name and an optional initial balance.
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The withdrawal amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.
- **Display Balance**: Print the current balance and the account owner's name.

Your program should handle multiple operations on a single account. For simplicity, assume all inputs are valid and formatted as specified.

**Input:**
- The first line contains the owner's name and an optional initial balance (default is 0.0).
- The subsequent lines contain operations in the format:
  - `deposit <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount.
  - `withdraw <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount.
  - `balance`: Display the current balance and account owner's name.

**Output:**
- For each `deposit` and `withdraw` operation, print a confirmation message.
- For each `balance` operation, print the account owner's name and the current balance.

**Sample Input:**
```
John Doe 100.0
deposit 50
withdraw 30
balance
withdraw 150
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Added 50 to the balance.
Withdrew 30 from the balance.
Account owner: John Doe
Account balance: 120.0
Insufficient funds.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a bank account with an owner and an initial balance.
        
        :param owner: The name of the account owner
        :param balance: The initial balance of the account, default is 0.0
        """
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit money into the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit, must be positive
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Added {amount} to the balance.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw money from the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw, must be positive and less than or equal to the current balance
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount} from the balance.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Display the current balance of the bank account.
        """
        print(f"Account owner: {self.owner}")
        print(f"Account balance: {self.balance}")

# Example usage:
# owner_info = input().split()
# owner = owner_info[0]

<think>
Okay, let's try to figure out how to solve this problem. So, the question is: Find the number of ways to select \( n \) objects from \( 3n \) objects, where \( n \) of the objects are identical and the remaining \( 2n \) are all different. Hmm, let's break this down.

First, we have a total of \( 3n \) objects. Among these, \( n \) are identical, so they're all the same, like n copies of a book, maybe. The other \( 2n \) are all distinct, so each one is unique. We need to select \( n \) objects from these \( 3n \). The challenge is that some of them are identical, so we have to account for that in our counting.

Let me start by thinking about how the selection can be made. Since there are identical objects, the number of ways to choose them might depend on how many of the identical ones we include in our selection. For example, if we take k of the identical objects, then we need to take \( n - k \) of the distinct objects. Since the identical objects are indistinct, once we choose how many to take, their order or which ones don't matter. But the distinct objects, each choice is unique.

So maybe the strategy is to consider different cases based on how many identical objects are selected. Then, sum over all possible numbers of identical objects we could take. That sounds like a standard combinatorial approach.

Let me formalize that. Let’s say we take \( k \) identical objects. Then the remaining \( n - k \) objects must come from the \( 2n \) distinct ones. The number of ways to choose these \( n - k \) distinct objects is \( \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \).

But how many values can \( k \) take? Since we can't take a negative number of identical objects or more than the available \( n \), so \( k \) can range from 0 to \( n \). Wait, but also, when we take \( n - k \) from the distinct ones, \( n - k \) can’t be negative. So, \( n - k \geq 0 \) implies \( k \leq n \). Also, since we can't take more than the \( n \) identical objects, \( k \leq n \), and obviously \( k \geq 0 \). So yes, \( k \) ranges from 0 to \( n \).

Therefore, the total number of ways should be the sum over \( k = 0 \) to \( k = n \) of \( \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \). Wait, but is that correct?

Wait, no. Wait a second. Wait, if we take \( k \) identical objects, the number of ways to take those is 1 (since they are identical), and then the number of ways to take the distinct objects is \( \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \). So, the total number of ways for each k is \( 1 \times \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \), and then we sum over k from 0 to n. So the total number is \( \sum_{k=0}^{n} \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \).

But another way to think about this sum is to note that \( \sum_{k=0}^{n} \dbinom{2n}{n - k} = \sum_{m=0}^{n} \dbinom{2n}{m} \), where m = n - k. When k goes from 0 to n, m goes from n to 0, but since the binomial coefficients are symmetric, \( \dbinom{2n}{m} = \dbinom{2n}{2n - m} \), so the sum from m=0 to n is equal to the sum from m= n to 2n. Wait, no, actually, if we sum m from 0 to n, that's the same as summing m from n down to 0. But the entire sum from m=0 to 2n is \( 2^{2n} \), which is the total number of subsets of a set of size 2n. But our sum is half of that? Wait, no, not exactly. Because the sum from m=0 to n of \( \dbinom{2n}{m} \) is not exactly half of \( 2^{2n} \), unless n is 0 or 1, maybe. Wait, for even numbers, the middle term is the largest, but the sum from 0 to n would be half of the total sum only if 2n is even? Hmm, maybe that's not the right approach.

Wait, but maybe we can find a closed-form expression for the sum \( \sum_{k=0}^{n} \dbinom{2n}{k} \). Is there a formula for this?

Alternatively, maybe there's a generating function approach. Let me think. The generating function for the number of ways to choose objects from the 2n distinct ones is \( (1 + x)^{2n} \). And since the identical objects can contribute any number from 0 to n (since we need total n objects), then the generating function for the identical objects is \( 1 + x + x^2 + \dots + x^n \). So the total generating function would be the product of these two: \( (1 + x + x^2 + \dots + x^n)(1 + x)^{2n} \). Then, the coefficient of \( x^n \) in this product would give the total number of ways. Hmm, that seems promising.

So, let's write that out. The generating function is \( \left( \sum_{k=0}^n x^k \right) \times (1 + x)^{2n} \). The sum \( \sum_{k=0}^n x^k \) is a geometric series, so it can be written as \( \frac{1 - x^{n + 1}}{1 - x} \). Therefore, the generating function becomes \( \frac{1 - x^{n + 1}}{1 - x} \times (1 + x)^{2n} \).

We need the coefficient of \( x^n \) in this product. Let's break it down:

First, \( \frac{1}{1 - x} \times (1 + x)^{2n} = (1 + x)^{2n} \times \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} x^m \). The coefficient of \( x^n \) in this would be the sum from m=0 to n of \( \dbinom{2n}{n - m} \), but maybe that's getting us back to the same sum as before. Wait, perhaps we need to consider the product as two terms: the first term is \( \frac{1}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \), which would give coefficients up to infinity, but then we subtract \( \frac{x^{n + 1}}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \), which would subtract the coefficients starting from \( x^{n + 1} \). Therefore, the coefficient of \( x^n \) in the original generating function would be the same as the coefficient of \( x^n \) in \( \frac{1}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \), minus the coefficient of \( x^n \) in \( \frac{x^{n + 1}}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \).

But the second term, \( \frac{x^{n + 1}}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \), when we take the coefficient of \( x^n \), since we have \( x^{n + 1} \) multiplied by \( \frac{(1 + x)^{2n}}{1 - x} \), which is \( x^{n + 1} \times \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} x^m \times (1 + x)^{2n} \). The coefficient of \( x^n \) in this product would require that \( n + 1 + m + k = n \), which implies \( m + k = -1 \), which is impossible since m and k are non-negative integers. Therefore, the coefficient of \( x^n \) in the second term is zero. So for each possible arrival/departure pair (Ken and Keiko), we can check if their intervals have an overlap of at least half an hour.

If yes, then the total cost would be Ken's cost to go to X and return, plus Keiko's. The minimal total cost among all such pairs is our answer.

So the plan is:

For each city X:

1. Find all possible arrival times (and corresponding departure times) Ken can have at X, along with the minimal cost for that path (KEN's path from H to X then to H, return by 18:00).

Then, for all pairs of arrival times (t_k, s_k) (for Ken and Keiko arriving at X), we check if their available intervals at X overlap by 30 minutes or more.

The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Initialization**: Create an employee with attributes `name`, `id`, `position`, and `salary`.
- **Details Retrieval**: Provide a method `get_details` that returns a formatted string containing all the employee's details.

- **Position Change**: Implement a method `promote` that updates the employee's position to a new specified position.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Initialization: `Employee("John Doe", 12345, "Software Engineer", 75000)`
- `get_details()` Output: `"Name: John Doe, ID: 12345, Position: Software Engineer, Salary: 75000"`
- `give_raise(10)` (after initialization)
- `get_details()` Output: `"Name: John Doe, ID: 12345, Position: Software Engineer, Salary: 82500"`
- `promote("Senior Software Engineer")`
- `get_details()` Output: `"Name: John Doe, ID: 12345, Position: Senior Software Engineer, Salary: 82500"`

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, id, position, salary):
        """
        Initialize an employee with the given name, id, position, and salary.
        
        :param name: str - The name of the employee.
        :param id: int - The unique identifier of the employee.
        :param position: str - The current position of the employee.
        :param salary: float - The current salary of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.id = id
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary
    
    def get_details(self):
        """
        Return a string containing all the employee's details.
        
        :return: str - Formatted string with employee details.
        """
        return f"Name: {self.name}, ID: {self.id}, Position: {self.position}, Salary: {self.salary}"
    
    def give_raise(self, percentage):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by a given percentage.
        
        :param percentage: float - The percentage by which to increase the salary.
        """
        if percentage > 0:
            self.salary += self.salary * (percentage / 100)
    
    def promote(self, new_position):
        """
        Change the employee's position to a new position.
        
        :param new_position: str - The new position of the employee.
        """
        self.position = new_position

The coefficient of \( x^n \) in this product would require that \( n + 1 + m + k = n \), which implies \( m + k = -1 \), which is impossible since m and k are non-negative integers. Therefore, the coefficient of \( x^n \) in the original generating function is equal to the coefficient of \( x^n \) in \( \frac{1}{1 - x}(1 + x)^{2n} \).

So, total ways for each k is \( \dbinom{2n}{n - k} \), and we sum from k=0 to k=n.

But this sum is the same as \( \sum_{k=0}^n \dbinom{2n}{n - k} = \sum_{m=0}^n \dbinom{2n}{m} \), where m = n - k. So yes, the sum becomes the sum of the first n + 1 binomial coefficients of \( \dbinom{2n}{m} \), from m=0 to m=n.

The total cost is the cost of Ken getting there and back, plus Keiko's cost of going to X and coming back. We need the minimal of this sum over all X and overlapping intervals.

Thus, perhaps the steps are:

1. For each city X:

a. Compute all possible arrival times for Ken into X such that he can return to H by 18:00. For each arrival time, we also need the minimal cost to get there and the latest possible departure time (so their interval is [arrival, T_k], where T_k is latest departure time from X possible without getting back after 18:00.

b. Do the same for Keiko (from Tokyo to X and return, time constraints).

2. For each city X, and each pair of Ken's interval (A_k, T_k) and Keiko's (A_kei, T_kei), check if the intervals overlap by at least 30 mins. For such pairs, the total cost is cost_ken + cost_kei. 

3. Find the minimal such sum over all valid pairs and all cities.

Now the challenge is to compute step 1 for each city efficiently.

So, let's think about how to compute step 1a (for Ken):

We can represent Ken's journey as any path starting from H at a departure time >=08:00, arriving at X at various times, and for each arrival time, we can also find the earliest time he can return home such that arrival at H is by 18:00.

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
  - `pages`: An integer representing the number of pages in the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.

- **Methods:**
  - `get_info()`: Returns a formatted string containing the book's title, author, year, number of pages, and ISBN.
  - `is_author(author_name)`: Takes a string `author_name` and returns `True` if the book's author matches `author_name` (case-insensitive), otherwise returns `False`.
  - `update_pages(new_pages)`: Updates the number of pages in the book to `new_pages` if `new_pages` is greater than zero; otherwise, raises a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `example_book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, 328, "9780451524935")`
- Output:
  - `example_book.get_info()` should return `"Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Year: 1949, Pages: 328, ISBN: 9780451524935"`
  - `example_book.is_author("George Orwell")` should return `True`
  - `example_book.is_author("george orwell")` should return `True`
  - `example_book.update_pages(350)` followed by `example_book.get_info()` should return `"Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Year: 1949, Pages: 350, ISBN: 9780451524935"`

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year, pages, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param pages: The number of pages in the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year = year
        self.pages = pages
        self.isbn = isbn

    def get_info(self):
        """
        Returns a formatted string with the book's information.

        :return: A string containing the book's title, author, year, pages, and ISBN.
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Year: {self.year}, Pages: {self.pages}, ISBN: {self.isbn}"

    def is_author(self, author_name):
        """
        Checks if the book's author matches the provided author_name (case-insensitive).

        :param author_name: The name of the author to check.
        :return: True if the author matches, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.author.lower() == author_name.lower()

    def update_pages(self, new_pages):
        """
        Updates the number of pages in the book.

        :param new_pages: The new number of pages.
        :raises ValueError: If new_pages is not greater than zero.
        """
        if new_pages > 0:
            self.pages = new_pages
        else:
            raise ValueError("The number of pages must be greater than zero.")

# Example usage
example_book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, 328, "9780451524935")
print(example_book.get_info())
print(example_book.is_author("George Orwell"))
print(example_book.is_author("george orwell"))
example_book.update_pages(350)
print(example_book.get_info())
```<#sep#> 42. Correct. So the formula works.

</think>To find the number of ways to select \( n \) objects from \( 3n \) objects, where \( n \) of the objects are identical and the remaining \( 2n \) objects are all different, we can proceed as follows:

1. **Identify the Selection Process**:
   - We need to select \( n \) objects from a total of \( 3n \) objects.
   - Among these \( 3n \) objects, \( n \) are identical, and \( 2n \) are distinct.

2. **Break Down the Selection**:
   - Let \( k \) be the number of identical objects selected. Then, \( k \) can range from \( 0 \) to \( n \).
   - The remaining \( n - k \) objects must be selected from the \( 2n \) distinct objects.

3. **Calculate the Number of Ways**:
   - For each \( k \), the number of ways to choose \( n - k \) distinct objects from \( 2n \) distinct objects is given by \( \binom{2n}{n - k} \).
   - Since the identical objects are indistinguishable, there is only 1 way to choose \( k \) identical objects.

4. **Sum Over All Possible Values of \( k \)**:
   - The total number of ways to select \( n \) objects is the sum of the number of ways for each \( k \):
     \[
     \sum_{k=0}^{n} \binom{2n}{n - k}
     \]

5. **Simplify the Sum**:
   - Using the symmetry of binomial coefficients, we know that \( \binom{2n}{n - k} = \binom{2n}{k} \).
   - Therefore, the sum can be rewritten as:
     \[
     \sum_{k=0}^{n} \binom{2n}{k}
     \]

6. **Use Combinatorial Identity**:
   - The sum of binomial coefficients from \( 0 \) to \( n \) for \( 2n \) is given by:
     \[
     \sum_{k=0}^{n} \binom{2n}{k} = \frac{1}{2} \left( 2^{2n} + \binom{2n}{n} \right)
     \]
   - This identity comes from the fact that the total sum of binomial coefficients \( \sum_{k=0}^{2n} \binom{2n}{k} = 2^{2n} \), and the symmetry of binomial coefficients around the middle term \( \binom{2n}{n} \).

7. **Final Answer**:
   - Therefore, the number of ways to select \( n \) objects from \( 3n \) objects, where \( n \) are identical and \( 2n \) are distinct, is:
     \[
     \boxed{\frac{2^{2n} + \binom{2n}{n}}{2}}
     \]<#sep#> and all cities.

```python

Arithmetic Progression, also known as arithmetic sequence, is a fundamental concept in mathematics that you may have encountered at some point in your academic journey. But do you truly understand its concept and fundamental principles?

Whether you are a student studying mathematics or someone looking to refresh your knowledge, this article will take you on a journey to explore the depths of arithmetic progression. From its definition to its various properties and applications, you will gain a comprehensive understanding of this mathematical concept.

So, let’s delve into the world of arithmetic progression, where numbers reveal patterns and hold secrets waiting to be unraveled. Are you ready?

### Key Takeaways:

• Arithmetic Progression, also known as arithmetic sequence, is an essential concept in mathematics.
• This article will guide you through the concept of arithmetic progression, from its definition to its various properties and applications.
• Understanding arithmetic progression will enhance your problem-solving skills and mathematical reasoning abilities.
• Arithmetic progression is not only relevant in mathematics but also has real-world applications in fields like finance, physics, and computer science.
• Stay tuned as we explore the fascinating world of arithmetic progression, one step at a time!

## Definition of Arithmetic Progression

An arithmetic progression, commonly known as an arithmetic sequence, is a sequence of numbers in which the difference between any two consecutive terms is constant. In other words, it is a sequence of numbers where each term is obtained by adding a fixed number, called the common difference, to the previous term.

For example, consider the sequence: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10. The common difference between consecutive terms is 2, as each term is obtained by adding 2 to the previous term.

Arithmetic progressions are widely used in various fields, including mathematics, economics, physics, and computer science. They play a crucial role in understanding patterns, solving problems, and making predictions.

“In an arithmetic progression, the common difference remains constant.”

## Formula for Arithmetic Progression

Arithmetic progression is a fundamental concept in mathematics that follows a specific pattern. One of the key aspects of arithmetic progression is the ability to calculate any term in the sequence using a formula.

The formula to find the nth term of an arithmetic progression is:

an = a1 + (n – 1)d

Where an is the nth term of the arithmetic progression, a1 is the first term, n is the position of the term in the sequence, and d is the common difference between consecutive terms.

This formula allows us to calculate any term in the arithmetic progression without having to manually generate each term in the sequence. It provides a convenient and efficient way to determine the value of a specific term based on its position and the common difference.

n an
1 a1
2 a1 + d
3 a1 + 2d
4 a1 + 3d

In the table above, each row represents a different term in the arithmetic progression. The first term, a1, is the starting point of the sequence. The common difference, d, determines the change between consecutive terms. By using the formula, we can calculate the value of any term in the sequence.

## Common Difference in Arithmetic Progression

An arithmetic progression is characterized by a constant difference between consecutive terms. This constant difference is known as the common difference. It plays a crucial role in determining the progression’s pattern and properties.

Let’s consider an example to understand the concept better:

### Example:

In an arithmetic progression, the first term (a1) is 2, and the common difference (d) is 4. Find the fifth term (a5).

To determine the fifth term, we can use the formula:

an = a1 + (n – 1) * d

Substituting the given values:

a5 = 2 + (5 – 1) * 4

a5 = 2 + 4 * 4

a5 = 2 + 16

a5 = 18

Therefore, the fifth term of the arithmetic progression is 18. This example demonstrates how the common difference enables us to generate specific terms in the progression.

The common difference provides valuable insights into the arithmetic progression’s behavior. It determines the rate of change between consecutive terms, ensuring a consistent increase or decrease throughout the sequence. Moreover, it helps identify the pattern and predict future terms in the progression.

Common Difference Progression Pattern
Positive (d > 0) The terms increase as the progression advances.
Negative (d The terms decrease as the progression advances.
Zero (d = 0) The terms remain constant throughout the progression.

Understanding the common difference is essential for comprehending the properties and applications of arithmetic progressions. It allows us to analyze and manipulate the sequence efficiently, making arithmetic progressions a fundamental concept in mathematics.

## Generating Terms in an Arithmetic Progression

In an arithmetic progression, the terms are generated by a specific formula. This formula allows us to easily find any term in the progression without having to calculate each term individually. By understanding how to generate terms in an arithmetic progression, we can efficiently analyze patterns and make predictions.

The formula for generating terms in an arithmetic progression is:

Tn = a + (n – 1)d

Tn represents the n-th term in the arithmetic progression.
a is the first term of the progression.
d is the common difference between consecutive terms.
n denotes the position of the term we want to find.

This formula enables us to calculate the value of any term in the progression by substituting the appropriate values for a, d, and n. Let’s take a closer look at how this formula works with an example:

### Example:

Consider an arithmetic progression with a first term of 3 and a common difference of 2. What is the value of the 5th term?

To find the 5th term, we use the formula:

T5 = a + (5 – 1)d

Substituting the given values:

T5 = 3 + (5 – 1)2

T5 = 3 + 4 * 2

T5 = 3 + 8

T5 = 11

Therefore, the 5th term in the arithmetic progression is 11.

By using the formula for generating terms in an arithmetic progression, we can easily find the value of any term based on its position in the sequence. This enables us to analyze and understand the patterns within the progression more effectively.

## Properties of Arithmetic Progression

Arithmetic progression exhibits several interesting properties that allow for better understanding and analysis of its behavior. By studying these properties, one can gain valuable insights into the sum of the terms and the number of terms in an arithmetic progression.

### Sum of Terms in Arithmetic Progression

One key property of arithmetic progression relates to finding the sum of its terms. The sum of an arithmetic progression can be calculated using the following formula:

Sum = (n/2) * [2a + (n-1)d]

Where:

• n represents the number of terms in the arithmetic progression
• a is the first term of the progression
• d denotes the common difference between consecutive terms

This formula allows for efficient computation of the sum, saving valuable time in various mathematical and real-world applications where arithmetic progressions are utilized.

### Number of Terms in Arithmetic Progression

Another important property of arithmetic progression is determining the number of terms present. The formula for calculating the number of terms is:

n = (l – a + d)/d

Where:

• n represents the number of terms
• a is the first term of the arithmetic progression
• d denotes the common difference between consecutive terms
• l represents the last term of the arithmetic progression

Knowing the number of terms in an arithmetic progression is essential for making accurate calculations and predictions, especially when dealing with real-life scenarios that involve sequences.

### Example:

Arithmetic Progression First Term (a) Common Difference (d) Last Term (l) Number of Terms (n) Sum of Terms
2, 5, 8, 11, 14 2 3 14 5 40

In the given example, the arithmetic progression starts with the first term of 2 and has a common difference of 3. The last term is 14, and the number of terms is 5. Hence, the sum of the terms equals 40.

Understanding the properties of arithmetic progression enhances one’s ability to analyze and manipulate sequences in mathematics, finance, and various other fields.

## Arithmetic Mean in Arithmetic Progression

In an arithmetic progression, the arithmetic mean plays a crucial role in understanding the pattern of the sequence. The arithmetic mean refers to the average of all the terms in the progression. It can be calculated by adding up all the terms and dividing the sum by the total number of terms.

The arithmetic mean is often denoted by the symbol ‘A’ or ‘M’ and is an essential concept in mathematics and various real-world applications.

To find the arithmetic mean, you can use the following formula:

Arithmetic Mean (A) = Sum of Terms / Number of Terms

Here is an example to illustrate the calculation of the arithmetic mean in an arithmetic progression:

1. Consider an arithmetic progression with the first term (a) equal to 1, common difference (d) equal to 2, and a total of 5 terms<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple vending machine simulation. The vending machine has a predefined set of items, each with a name, price, and quantity. Your program should allow a user to select an item, make a payment, and receive the item if the payment is sufficient and the item is in stock. If the item is not available or the payment is insufficient, the program should provide appropriate feedback.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 10), the number of items in the vending machine.
- The next `n` lines each contain three space-separated values: the name of the item (a string of up to 20 characters), the price of the item (a float with up to 2 decimal places), and the quantity of the item (an integer).
- The following lines represent user interactions with the vending machine. Each line contains either:
  - A single integer `k` (1 ≤ k ≤ n), representing the user's choice of item.
  - The string "q", indicating the user wants to quit.
- If the user selects an item, the next line contains a float representing the payment made by the user.

**Output:**
- For each user interaction, print the appropriate message based on the user's action:
  - If the user selects an item that is out of stock, print "Sorry, [item name] is out of stock."
  - If the user provides insufficient funds, print "Insufficient funds."
  - If the user provides more than the required amount, print "这里是你的找零: $[change]".
  - If the user provides the exact amount, print "Thank you for your purchase. Here is your [item name]!".
  - If the user enters "q", print "Goodbye!" and terminate the program.

**Sample Input:**
```
3
Soda 1.50 3
Chips 1.00 2
Candy 0.50 5
1
1.50
2
0.50
3
q
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Welcome to the Vending Machine!
Please select an item:
1. Soda ($1.50) - 3 in stock
2. Chips ($1.00) - 2 in stock
3. Candy ($0.50) - 5 in stock

Welcome to the Vending Machine!
Please select an item:
1. Soda ($1.50) - 2 in stock
2. Chips ($1.00) - 2 in stock
3. Candy ($0.50) - 5 in stock
Enter the number of your choice (or 'q' to quit): Thank you for your purchase. Here is your Candy!
Welcome to the Vending Machine!
Please select an item:
1. Soda ($1.50) - 2 in stock
2. Chips ($1.00) - 2 in stock
3. Candy ($0.50) - 4 in stock
Enter the number of your choice (or 'q' to quit): Goodbye!
```

```python
class VendingMachine:
    def __init__(self, items):
        """
        Initialize the vending machine with a list of items.
        
        :param items: List of dictionaries, each containing 'name', 'price', and 'quantity' of an item.
        """
        self.items = items

    def menu(self):
        """
        Display the menu and handle user interactions.
        """
        print("Welcome to the Vending Machine!")
        print("Please select an item:")
        for index, item in enumerate(self.items, start=1):
            print(f"{index}. {item['name']} (${item['price']:.2f}) - {item['quantity']} in stock")
        choice = input("Enter the number of your choice (or 'q' to quit): ")
        if choice.lower() == 'q':
            print("Goodbye!")
            return
        try:
            choice_index = int(choice) - 1
            if choice_index < 0 or choice_index >= len(self.items):
                raise ValueError("Invalid choice.")
            selected_item = self.items[choice_index]
            if selected_item['quantity'] == 0:
                print(f"Sorry, {selected_item['name']} is out of stock.")
            else:
                self.make_purchase(selected_item)
        except ValueError:
            print("Invalid input. Please enter a valid number.")
        self.menu()  # Repeat the menu until user quits

    def make_purchase(self, item):
        """
        Handle the purchase of an item.
        
        :param item: Dictionary containing 'name', 'price', and 'quantity' of the selected item.
        """
        payment = float(input(f"Please insert ${item['price']:.2f} for {item['name']}: "))
        if payment < item['price']:
            print("Insufficient funds.")
        elif payment > item['price']:
            print(f"这里是你的找零: ${payment - item['price']:.2f}")
        item['quantity'] -= 1
        print(f"Thank you for your purchase. Here is your {item['name']}!")

# Read input and run the vending machine program
n = int(input())
items = [input().split() for _ in range(n)]
items = [{"name": item[0], "price": float(item[1]), "quantity": int(item[2])} for item in items]
vm = VendingMachine(items)
vm.menu()

<#sep#> calculated by adding up all the terms and dividing the sum by the total number of terms.

The arithmetic mean is often denoted by the symbol ‘A’ or ‘M’ and is an essential concept in mathematics and various real-world applications.

To find the arithmetic mean, you can use the following formula:

Arithmetic Mean (A) = Sum of Terms / Number of Terms

Here is an example to illustrate the calculation of the arithmetic mean in an arithmetic progression:

1. Consider an arithmetic progression with the first term (a) equal to 1, common difference (d) equal to 2, and a total of 5 terms.
2. Using the formula mentioned earlier, we can find the sum of the terms.
Term Value
First Term (a) 1
Last Term (l) 9
Number of Terms (n) 5
Sum of Terms (S) 25
Arithmetic Mean (A) 5

In the above example, the sum of the terms is 25, and the number of terms is 5. Dividing the sum by the number of terms gives us an arithmetic mean of 5.

The arithmetic mean provides valuable insights into the central tendency of the arithmetic progression. It helps identify the average value of the terms, allowing for a better understanding of the overall pattern and behavior of the sequence.

## Finding the Sum of an Arithmetic Progression

Calculating the sum of an arithmetic progression can be done efficiently using a simple formula derived from the fundamental principles of the sequence. The sum provides valuable insights into the overall magnitude of the progression and is often essential in various mathematical calculations and real-world applications.

To find the sum of the terms in an arithmetic progression, a formula known as the arithmetic series formula is employed. The formula allows us to calculate the sum quickly without the need to manually add each term.

Arithmetic Series Formula: The sum (S) of an arithmetic progression with first term (a), common difference (d), and a total of (n) terms can be calculated using the following formula:

S = (n/2) * [2a + (n-1)d]

By substituting the values of the first term (a), common difference (d), and the number of terms (n) into the formula, we can effortlessly determine the sum of the arithmetic progression. This formula is derived from the concept of pairing the terms at the beginning and end of the sequence, which simplifies the calculation process.

Let’s consider an example to illustrate the application of the arithmetic series formula:

Example: 4 3 6

In this example, we have a first term (a) of 4, a common difference (d) of 3, and a total of 6 terms (n). To find the sum (S), we will substitute these values into the formula:

S = (6/2) * [2(4) + (6-1)(3)]

S = 3 * [8 + 5(3)]

S = 3 * [8 + 15]

S = 3 * 23

S = 69

Therefore, in this particular arithmetic progression, the sum of the terms is 69.

The arithmetic series formula provides a streamlined approach to calculating the sum of arithmetic progressions, enabling mathematicians, students, and professionals to efficiently work with large sequences and make accurate calculations. Understanding how to use this formula is crucial for solving problems involving arithmetic progressions and analyzing their properties.

## Finding the Number of Terms in an Arithmetic Progression

In an arithmetic progression, knowing the number of terms is crucial for various calculations and analyses. By understanding the formula, you can determine this value accurately and efficiently.

“The number of terms in an arithmetic progression can be found using the formula:

n = (an – a1) / d + 1

where:

• n is the number of terms
• an is the last term
• a1 is the first term
• d is the common difference

Let’s consider an example:

an a1 d n
50 10 5 ?

To find n, we can use the following steps:

1. Calculate an – a1: 50 – 10 = 40
2. Divide the result by d: 40 / 5 = 8
3. Add 1 to the quotient: 8 + 1 = 9

Therefore, in this specific case, the number of terms (n) in the arithmetic progression is 9. By applying the formula, you can easily determine the number of terms in any arithmetic progression.

## Finding the Last Term in an Arithmetic Progression

In an arithmetic progression, the last term refers to the final value or element of the sequence. It is the term that appears at the end of the progression.

There are different methods to determine the last term in an arithmetic progression, depending on the given information. The most common approach is to use the formula for the nth term of an arithmetic progression, which is:

an = a1 + (n – 1)d

Where:

• an represents the nth term of the progression
• a1 is the first term of the progression
• n is the number of terms
• d is the common difference

By substituting the values of a1, n, and d into the formula, you can easily calculate the last term. Let’s consider an example:

Example:

Given an arithmetic progression with a first term (a1) of 3, a common difference (d) of 5, and a total number of terms (n) of 10. To find the last term (an), we can use the formula:

an = a1 + (n – 1)d

Substituting the values into the formula yields:

an = 3 + (10 – 1) * 5

Simplifying the equation further:

an = 3 + 9 * 5

an = 3 + 45

an = 48

Therefore, the last term of the arithmetic progression is 48.

Using the formula simplifies the process of finding the last term in an arithmetic progression. However, it is important to note that you can also find the last term by examining the pattern of the progression or using other methods based on the given information.

## Special Cases of Arithmetic Progression

Arithmetic progression is a fundamental concept in mathematics, but there are special cases that require further exploration. Let’s delve into these special cases and understand their implications.

### Case 1: Common Difference is Zero

When the common difference in an arithmetic progression is zero, each term in the sequence will be equal to the first term. This means that all terms will have the same value. For example, if the first term is a and the common difference is 0, the nth term of the arithmetic progression can be calculated as:

an = a

This special case results in a constant sequence where all the terms are identical. It is essential to recognize this special case, as it can have significant implications when dealing with arithmetic progressions.

### Case 2: Common Difference is Negative

When the common difference in an arithmetic progression is negative, it leads to a unique sequence. The terms in the sequence will decrease as we move along the progression. For example, if the first term is a and the common difference is d (where d < 0), the nth term of the arithmetic progression can be calculated as:

an = a + (n-1)d

In this case, the terms progressively become smaller, resulting in a descending sequence.

Understanding these special cases helps deepen our comprehension of arithmetic progression and its diverse applications. By mastering the different scenarios, mathematicians can confidently tackle a wide range of problems and explore the subject’s inherent complexities.

Special Case Scenario
Common Difference is Zero All terms in the sequence are the same
Common Difference is Negative The terms in the sequence decrease progressively

## Applications of Arithmetic Progression

Arithmetic progression, also known as arithmetic sequence, finds extensive applications in various fields due to its predictable and ordered nature. Its linear progression allows for easy calculation and analysis, making it valuable in numerous real-world scenarios.

### Finance and Economics

In finance and economics, arithmetic progression is utilized to model and analyze financial growth, investments, and loan repayments. It helps in forecasting trends, calculating interest rates, and determining future values. Banks, investors, and financial institutions rely on arithmetic progression to make informed decisions and manage financial transactions effectively.

### Population Studies

Arithmetic progression plays a crucial role in population studies and demographic analyses. It helps researchers predict population growth, estimate future populations, and analyze migration patterns. Governments and organizations make use of this mathematical concept to plan infrastructure development, allocate resources, and address social needs.

### Computer Science

In computer science, arithmetic progression finds applications in algorithms and data structures. It is used to perform efficient searching, indexing, and sorting operations. Arithmetic progression is also utilized in programming loops to iterate through a sequence of values, providing a systematic approach to solving problems.

### Physics and Engineering

Arithmetic progression is employed in physics and engineering to model and analyze physical phenomena. It helps in understanding the motion of objects, calculating acceleration, and predicting future positions. Electrical engineers use arithmetic progression to design circuits, while civil engineers utilize it for structural analysis and design.
