Experiment 15: ATOMIC SPECTRA

PURPOSE:

The purpose of this experiment is to measure the wavelengths of light emitted by atoms of
different elements.

INTRODUCTION:

The electrons of gases can be raised to excited states if the atoms of the gas absorb
specific quanta of energy. The electrons are said to have been raised from their ground state to
higher energy levels. When these electrons fall back to the ground state or to another lower level,
light is emitted. These photons have unique wavelengths corresponding to the difference in
energy between the two states of the electron as it falls.
In this experiment, high voltage supplies the energy to the atoms in the gas discharge tube.
The electrons are excited and fall to a lower state almost immediately. The mixture of light
produced can be separated using a diffraction grating and then the wavelength can be calculated
from the equation = d sin.

SUPPLIES & EQUIPMENT:

Spectrum tube power supply         Ar, He, H Spectrum tubes           2 ringstands
2 One-meter sticks                 2 Buret clamps                     Grating
Grating holder & stand             Small reading lamp

PROCEDURE:

1. Set up the apparatus as shown in Figure 2.

2. Adjust your eyes in a position such that you can locate the first order spectral lines.

3. Determine the x and L for each spectral line for argon, helium and hydrogen

L 
4. Determine tan  = x and  = tan-1 x .
L

5. Determine the wavelength,  of the spectral lines. The grating has 600 grooves per millimeter.
The grating constant, d, is the distance between the grooves on the grating. For our gratings,
d = 6001000 in units of nanometers.

6. Compare these wavelengths with the known spectral line values given.

The configuration file must adhere to a specific structure. The configuration should include the following keys: `app_name` (a string), `server_port` (an integer), `debug_mode` (a boolean), and `database_config` (a dictionary containing `host` (a string), `port` (an integer), and `name` (a string)). Your script should load the configuration from a file named `config.json`, validate its structure, and print an error message if the structure is invalid. If the file is not found or contains invalid JSON, appropriate error messages should be displayed.

**Sample Input (config.json):**
```json
{
    "app_name": "MyApp",
    "server_port": 8080,
    "debug_mode": true,
    "database_config": {
        "host": "localhost",
        "port": 5432,
        "name": "mydatabase"
    }
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```
(No output, as the configuration is valid)
```

**Sample Input (config.json):**
```json
{
    "app_name": "MyApp",
    "server_port": "8080",
    "debug_mode": true,
    "database_config": {
        "host": "localhost",
        "port": 5432,
        "name": "mydatabase"
    }
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Invalid configuration: server_port should be an integer
```

```python
import json

def validate_config(config):
    """
    Validates the structure of the configuration dictionary.
    
    Args:
        config (dict): The configuration dictionary to validate.
    
    Returns:
        tuple: A tuple containing a boolean indicating if the configuration is valid,
               and an error message if the configuration is invalid.
    """
    if not isinstance(config, dict):
        return False, "Configuration is not a dictionary"

    required_keys = {'app_name', 'server_port', 'debug_mode', 'database_config'}
    missing_keys = required_keys - config.keys()
    if missing_keys:
        return False, f"Missing keys in configuration: {', '.join(missing_keys)}"

    if not isinstance(config['app_name'], str):
        return False, "app_name should be a string"

    if not isinstance(config['server_port'], int):
        return False, "server_port should be an integer"

    if not isinstance(config['debug_mode'], bool):
        return False, "debug_mode should be a boolean"

    if not isinstance(config['database_config'], dict):
        return False, "database_config should be a dictionary"

    db_config_required_keys = {'host', 'port', 'name'}
    missing_db_config_keys = db_config_required_keys - config['database_config'].keys()
    if missing_db_config_keys:
        return False, f"Missing keys in database_config: {', '.join(missing_db_config_keys)}"

    if not isinstance(config['database_config']['host'], str):
        return False, "Host should be a string in database_config"

    if not isinstance(config['database_config']['port'], int):
        return False, "Port should be an integer in database_config"

    if not isinstance(config['database_config']['name'], str):
        return False, "Name should be a string in database_config"

    return True, None

def main():
    """
    Main function to load and validate the configuration from a JSON file.
    """
    config_file_path = 'config.json'
    try:
        with open(config_file_path, 'r') as file:
            config = json.load(file)
    except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
        print(f"Error decoding JSON: {e}")
        return
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"File {config_file_path} not found")
        return
    
    is_valid, error_message = validate_config(config)
    if not is_valid:
        print(f"Invalid configuration: {error_message}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```<#sep#> mod125=123 mod125.

+    def export_api(self, rest_api_id: str, export_type: str) -> Dict[str, Any]:
+        """
+        Not all fields are implemented yet.
+        The export-type is currently ignored - we will only return the 'swagger'-format
+        """
+        try:
+            api = self.get_rest_api(rest_api_id)
+        except RestAPINotFound:
+            raise StageNotFoundException
+        if export_type not in ["swagger", "oas30"]:
+            raise BadRequestException(f"No API exporter for type '{export_type}'")

+            "swagger": "2.0",
+            "info": {"version": now, "title": api.name},
+            "host": f"{api.id}.execute-api.{self.region_name}.amazonaws.com",
+            "basePath": "/",
+            "schemes": ["https"],
+            "paths": {},
+            "definitions": {"Empty": {"type": "object", "title": "Empty Schema"}},
+        }
+        for res in api.resources.values():
+            path = res.get_path()
+            resp["paths"][path] = {}
+            for method_type, method in res.resource_methods.items():
+                resp["paths"][path][method_type] = {
+                    "produces": ["application/json"],
+                    "responses": {},
+                }
+                for code, _ in method.method_responses.items():
+                    resp["paths"][path][method_type]["responses"][code] = {
+                        "description": f"{code} response",
+                        "schema": {"$ref": "#/definitions/Empty"},
+                    }
+        return resp

8. Total internal reflection--rainbow and fiber optics

observations and explaining the principles behind each demonstration.

Apparatus Notes:

1. Soap bubble: 1 part glycerin, 4 parts clear detergent, 10 parts water. Specify position of parts
of setup with masking tape on table.

2. Hologram: Diffuse sodium light with ground glass screen to prevent glare. Use black shield
and black paper underneath.

3: Optical flats: Diffuse sodium light with ground glass screen to prevent glare. View from one-
half to one meter away.

5. Michelson's interferometer: Put screen at least 2 m away.

6. Single slit diffraction - positive and negative slit multiple slits. Project on blackboard across
room. Use 2 pieces of paper 1' x 2.5' for screens.

7. Polarized light: Use circular adjustable polaroid holder. Use blue battery charger set at 6V and
12 V Pasco lamp.

8. Total internal reflection: Use large (8") crystallizing dish from chemistry. Place screen about a
meter away. Use slit opening over lamp. Use a red battery charger set at 12V and 12 V
Pasco lamp. Shield apparatus from stray light. Use lab jack for laser.

Stats:
 views: 156<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a Python class hierarchy to model a simple banking system. The system should include a base class `Account` with methods for depositing and withdrawing funds. Additionally, create subclasses `SavingsAccount` and `CheckingAccount` that inherit from `Account` and implement additional behaviors unique to savings and checking accounts, such as interest calculation for savings accounts or overdraft fees for checking accounts.

**Input:**
- The input will be a series of commands to interact with the banking system. Each command will be on a new line and can be one of the following:
  - `Account <owner> <balance>`: Create a new `Account` object with the given owner and balance.
  - `SavingsAccount <owner> <balance> <interest_rate>`: Create a new `SavingsAccount` object with the given owner, balance, and interest rate.
  - `CheckingAccount <owner> <balance> <overdraft_fee>`: Create a new `CheckingAccount` object with the given owner, balance, and overdraft fee.
  - `deposit <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount into the current account.
  - `withdraw <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount from the current account.
  - `apply_interest`: Apply interest to the current `SavingsAccount`.
  - `print`: Print the current state of the account.

**Output:**
- For each `print` command, output the current state of the account in the format specified by the `__str__` method of the account class.

**Sample Input:**
```
Account John 1000
deposit 500
withdraw 200
withdraw 1500
print
SavingsAccount Jane 500 0.02
deposit 1000
apply_interest
print
CheckingAccount Jack 200 5
deposit 300
withdraw 600
print
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Account owner: John
Account balance: $300
Savings Account owner: Jane
Account balance: $1510.0
Interest Rate: 2.00%
Checking Account owner: Jack
Account balance: -$105.0
Overdraft Fee: $5
```

```python
class Account:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """Deposit a positive amount into the account."""
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Added {amount} to the balance")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """Withdraw a positive amount from the account if sufficient balance exists."""
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount} from the balance")
            else:
                print("Insufficient balance")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Account owner: {self.owner}\nAccount balance: ${self.balance}"


class SavingsAccount(Account):
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0, interest_rate=0.02):
        super().__init__(owner, balance)
        self.interest_rate = interest_rate

    def apply_interest(self):
        """Apply interest to the account balance."""
        interest = self.balance * self.interest_rate
        self.balance += interest
        print(f"Applied interest. New balance: ${self.balance}")

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Savings Account owner: {self.owner}\nAccount balance: ${self.balance:.1f}\nInterest Rate: {self.interest_rate:.2%}"


class CheckingAccount(Account):
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0, overdraft_fee=5):
        super().__init__(owner, balance)
        self.overdraft_fee = overdraft_fee

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """Withdraw a positive amount from the account, applying an overdraft fee if necessary."""
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount} from the balance")
            else:
                self.balance -= amount + self.overdraft_fee
                print(f"Insufficient balance, overdraft fee applied. New balance (after fee): ${self.balance}")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def __str__(self):
        return f"Checking Account owner: {self.owner}\nAccount balance: ${self.balance:.1f}\nOverdraft Fee: ${self.overdraft_fee}"

\boxed{248}
</think>To find the last three digits of the number \( N = 1998^{1999^{2000}} \), we need to compute \( N \mod 1000 \). We will use the Chinese Remainder Theorem by breaking it into modulo 8 and modulo 125.

### Step 1: Compute \( N \mod 8 \)
First, we find \( 1998 \mod 8 \):
\[
1998 \div 8 = 249 \text{ remainder } 6 \implies 1998 \equiv 6 \mod 8
\]
Next, we need to compute \( 6^{1999^{2000}} \mod 8 \). Notice that:
\[
6^1 \equiv 6 \mod 8, \quad 6^2 \equiv 4 \mod 8, \quad 6^3 \equiv 0 \mod 8
\]
Since \( 6^3 \equiv 0 \mod 8 \) and \( 1999^{2000} \) is a large number, we have:
\[
6^{1999^{2000}} \equiv 0 \mod 8
\]
Thus:
\[
N \equiv 0 \mod 8
\]

### Step 2: Compute \( N \mod 125 \)
Since 1998 and 125 are coprime, we use Euler's theorem. Euler's totient function \( \phi(125) = 100 \):
\[
1998^{100} \equiv 1 \mod 125
\]
We need to reduce the exponent \( 1999^{2000} \mod 100 \):
\[
1999 \equiv 99 \mod 100 \implies 99^{2000} \mod 100
\]
Since \( 99 \equiv -1 \mod 100 \):
\[
(-1)^{2000} \equiv 1 \mod 100
\]
Thus, the exponent reduces to 1 modulo 100. Therefore:
\[
1998^{1999^{2000}} \equiv 1998^1 \mod 125
\]
Compute \( 1998 \mod 125 \):
\[
1998 - 15 \times 125 = 1998 - 1875 = 123 \implies 1998 \equiv 123 \mod 125
\]
Hence:
\[
1998^{1999^{2000}} \equiv 123 \mod 125
\]

### Step 3: Combine Results Using Chinese Remainder Theorem
We need \( x \) such that:
\[
x \equiv 0 \mod 8 \quad \text{and} \quad x \equiv 123 \mod 125
\]
Let \( x = 125k + 123 \). Then:
\[
125k + 123 \equiv 0 \mod 8 \implies 5k + 3 \equiv 0 \mod 8 \implies 5k \equiv -3 \equiv 5 \mod 8
\]
Solving \( 5k \equiv 5 \mod 8 \):
\[
k \equiv 1 \mod 8 \implies k = 8m + 1
\]
Substituting back:
\[
x = 125(8m + 1) + 123 = 1000m + 248
\]
The smallest solution is \( x \equiv 248 \mod 1000 \).

Thus, the last three digits of \( N \) are \(\boxed{248}\).<#sep#> idea:

   a. Build a spanning tree of the county graph.

   b. Select 12 edges to cut, splitting the tree into 13 connected components.

   c. The districts are these components.

This can create exactly 13 connected districts. For each cut, we choose edges randomly to split until 13 districts. 

This could be a valid initial partition.

The steps would be:

- Build a spanning tree (undirected). 

- Randomly choose 12 edges in the tree, and "break" each edge, creating splits. The resulting components are the districts.

This way, each district is a connected subgraph (since they are connected in the original trees minus edges).

This could be a good basis for generating an initial partition.

This method ensures a valid partition since the spanning tree after edge removal will form exactly (1 +number of edges removed) components. So removing 12 edges from a spanning tree (which has N-1 edges, where N is number of counties) would give N-1 -12 edges, so the number of components is 12+1=13? Wait no.  

The number of connected components after cutting M edges in a tree (originally one component) is 1 + number of cut edges.

Yes, so cutting 12 edges gives 13 components exactly.

Therefore, this method can be used to randomly generate an initial valid partition.

So steps to create one: 

1. Find a spanning tree.

2. Select random edges in the tree and cut them until there 12 cuts (resulting in 13 districts).

The surviv3ring edges form the districts.

This requires that I can traverse the tree and choose edges to break randomly.

This is likely manageable.

Once initial partition is generated, then the local search can be applied:

   a. Select a random county.

   b. Find its current district D.

   c. Check the neighboring districts adjacent to this county (i.e., districts that share counties adjacent to this county).

   d. For each neighboring district N-D, consider moving this county from D to N-D, provided that after this, both D and N-D remain connected.

   How to check connectedness after moving?

After moving the county to N-D, we need to ensure that:

- The source D (now without this county) is still connected.

- The destination N-D (now with this county added) is still connected.

To compute this:

For the source D: the subset of its original counties minus the moved county must form a connected graph.

For the destination N-D: the subset is the original ND + the moved county must form a connected graph.

The moved county's adjacency must include at least one county in N-D, since it was a neighbor of the current county and part of N-D, so N-D must currently have a county adjacent to the moved county.

Therefore, after adding, N-D's counties plus the new one should still be connected. 

But perhaps this is too computationally intensive if checking connectedness each time is slow.

Hence, need a way for quickly checking connectivity.

Alternatively, after a swap, the move is only allowed if the county is adjacent to the target district (so it has an edge into that district), so when added, that district is still connected.

The source district becomes the previous D minus the county.

If the previous D minus the county is still connected, then allow the swap.

Thus, the check for D's remaining connected:

Check connectedness. 

For that, one could represent each district as a set of counties.

deleting a county — to see if the connected component remains.

To check that quickly requires maintaining for each district a adjacency graph, but it can be done with BFS.

So for each candidate move:

   - Extract the source district D and the candidate destination districtND.

   - Check whether the county is adjacent to any member of ND (the target district) — which must be true, otherwise can't do.

   - Create newSource = D - {county}

   - Check if newSource is connected.

   This is done via BFS: pick a starting node in newSource, and see if all other nodes are reachable.

   - Create newND = ND ∪ {county}

   - Check if newND is connected.

   - The move is valid only if both newSource and newND are connected.

   (at this point, we can also check whether the county wasn't the sole connection between parts of D, thus breaking it)

If the move is valid, then compute the new score.

If better than current, accept the move; else perhaps in Monte Carlo fashion decide whether to accept a worse move (Simulated Annealing style).

This seems computationally heavy however, because for each possible move, we have to do two BFS. But if the counties are on the order of maybe 100 (assuming NC has ~100 counties), then BFS on a district of maybe 10 counties takes small time. It could be manageable.

Alternatively, there's a better way.

Wait if the initial district D was connected, and the moved county is an articulation point, then removing it splits the district.

An articulation point is a node whose removal increases the number of connected components.

Thus, checking if the county is an articulation point in D. 

So step for D:

   - remove county from D.

   - check if the remaining counties form a connected set.

   -> equivalent to whether the county was an articulation point of D's graph.

   To check this:

      - One way is BFS from any remaining node, see if all other nodes are reachable.

      Alternatively, since D's adjacency list is a subset of NC's, we can represent the subgraph and run a BFS.

So for a district of size N, the check is O(N) time.

Assuming 100 counties and a move is 100 operations, each requiring two BFS steps.

So for N=100 county districts:

  - Two districts, each BFS ~100 steps each (though district can be smaller).

Total: per move, ~2*100 steps. For each move considered, the checks could be manageable.

Hence, this method might be feasible.

Therefore, the plan for the code is:

Implement the following algorithm:

1. Initialize:

   a. Read the county data and adjacency data.

   b. Choose p*.

   c. Compute for each county their contribution (delta = votes[p*] - votes[ other party? doesn't matter, just need to know majority).

   d. Find total_population = sum_{all c} (D_v[c] + R_v[c])

   e. ideal_avg = total_population / 13 as a benchmark.

2. Generate an initial partition using the Spanning Tree approach:

   i. Build a spanning tree of the adjacency graph.

   ii. Randomly choose 12 edges from the spanning tree to cut, generating exactly 13 districts.

3. Evaluate its score.

4. Perform a local search for improvement:

   a. For a number of iterations (e.g., thousands):

      i. Pick a random county c.

      ii. Determine its current district Dc.

      iii. Find all neighboring districts via c's adjacent counties (i.e., districts adjacent to c).

      iv. For each such neighbor district ND:

          - Check if moving c to ND is possible (i.e., 

             After the move:

             - Dc - c must be connected.

             - ND + c must be connected.

          v. Compute this possibility by checking the two conditions via BFS.

      v. Among all valid possible moves (steps iv), choose one (randomly, or the best scoring one if any).

      vi. Compute the score change resulting from swapping. 

      vii. If the new score is higher than previous, then accept the swap; else, maybe accept it probabilistically if in a simulated annealing approach.

      viii. Update the current partition accordingly.

   b. The annealing would involve allowing moves that temporarily decrease the score but might lead to a better global solution.

   c. Continue until a stopping condition (max iterations, no improvement for x steps, or sufficient time). 

The annealing temperature can be a parameter.

5. Return the best partition found.

Now, coding this would require:

Implementing the following functions:

- Loading and parsing the CSV files.

- Adjacency list for all counties.

- Function to compute spanning tree of a graph. How to build a spanning tree?

Well, one can do a BFS/DFS and track the tree as we build it. For example:

   a. Start with a random node (seed).

   b. Keep track of visited.

   c. While there are unvisited nodes:

      pick an edge from current to unvisited.

Once the spanning tree edges are built, they form a set of edges that connect all nodes without cycles.

The spanning tree is represented as edges between nodes.

   For the code, perhaps the easiest way is to represent edges as a list of (u, v).

Note that a spanning tree can be built via BFS:

   Initialize a list for tree edges.

   visited = set()

   queue = [seed]

   while queue not empty:

      current = queue.pop()

      for neighbor in adjacency<#sep#>S3 Select: gzip input and CSV output
I'm testing some code that uses S3 Select and have a need for two unsupported features:

- Gzip-encoded input (in my case the compressed content is CSV, although I don't think that matters)
- CSV output (right now it only outputs JSON)

I was able to get the gzip working pretty easily with this code snippet (in `s3/models.py:select_object_content`):

```python
if input_details.get('CompressionType') == 'GZIP':
    import gzip
    from io import BytesIO
    with gzip.open(BytesIO(key.value), 'rt') as f:
        query_input = f.read()
else:
    query_input = key.value.decode("utf-8")  # type: ignore

DOCUMENT INFO
Shared By:
Categories:
Tags:
Stats:
 views: 156 posted: 11/23/2011 language: English pages: 36<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that encapsulates the details of a book. The class should have the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `year_published`, and `genre`. Implement the following methods:

1. `__init__(self, title, author, year_published, genre)`: Initializes a new instance of the `Book` class with the given attributes.
2. `get_details(self)`: Returns a string representation of the book in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Year: {year_published}, Genre: {genre}".
3. `from_string(cls, book_string)`: A class method that constructs a `Book` object from a string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Year: {year_published}, Genre: {genre}". The method should raise a `ValueError` if the input string does not match the expected format.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Title: To Kill a Mockingbird, Author: Harper Lee, Year: 1960, Genre: Fiction"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"Title: To Kill a Mockingbird, Author: Harper Lee, Year: 1960, Genre: Fiction"
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year_published, genre):
        """
        Initializes a new instance of the Book class.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param year_published: The year the book was published.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_published = year_published
        self.genre = genre

    def get_details(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.

        :return: A string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Year: {year_published}, Genre: {genre}".
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Year: {self.year_published}, Genre: {self.genre}"

    @classmethod
    def from_string(cls, book_string):
        """
        Constructs a Book object from a string representation.

        :param book_string: A string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Year: {year_published}, Genre: {genre}".
        :return: A new Book object.
        :raises ValueError: If the input string is not in the correct format.
        """
        try:
            title, author, year, genre = book_string.split(', ')
            title = title.split(': ')[1]
            author = author.split(': ')[1]
            year = year.split(': ')[1]
            genre = genre.split(': ')[1]
            return cls(title, author, year, genre)
        except ValueError:
            raise ValueError("Input string is not in the correct format")
```<#sep#>Given the equations $(\log_2 a)(\log_4 b) + a^2 = 135$ and $(\log_4 a)(\log_2 b) + b^2 = 263$, find $p+q$ if $ab$ can be written as $p\sqrt{q}$.

<think>
Okay, let me try to solve this problem. So, we have two equations involving logarithms and squares of a and b. The goal is to find ab in the form p√q and then compute p + q. Alright, let's start by writing down the equations again to make sure I have them right.

First equation: (log₂ a)(log₄ b) + a² = 135

Second equation: (log₄ a)(log₂ b) + b² = 263

Hmm, both equations have logarithmic terms and quadratic terms. Maybe we can simplify the logarithmic parts first. Let's recall that log base 4 can be converted to log base 2 since 4 is 2 squared. The logarithm change of base formula says log₄ x = log₂ x / log₂ 4 = log₂ x / 2. So, log₄ x is just half of log₂ x. That might help simplify things.

Let me rewrite both equations using this conversion. Let's denote log₂ a as m and log₂ b as n. That might make the equations easier to handle. So:

log₄ a = (log₂ a)/2 = m/2

Similarly, log₄ b = (log₂ b)/2 = n/2

So substituting into the first equation:

(log₂ a)(log₄ b) + a² = (m)(n/2) + a² = (mn)/2 + a² = 135

Similarly, the second equation:

(log₄ a)(log₂ b) + b² = (m/2)(n) + b² = (mn)/2 + b² = 263

So now both equations have (mn)/2 plus a squared term. Let me write that out:

1) (mn)/2 + a² = 135

2) (mn)/2 + b² = 263

If I subtract the first equation from the second, I can eliminate the (mn)/2 term. Let's try that:

[(mn)/2 + b²] - [(mn)/2 + a²] = 263 - 135

Simplifying left side: (mn)/2 - (mn)/2 + b² - a² = 128

Which simplifies to b² - a² = 128

Hmm, that's a difference of squares. So, (b - a)(b + a) = 128

Not sure if that helps yet, but maybe we can use that later.

Now, looking back at the original equations, they both have (mn)/2. Let's denote mn/2 as some variable, maybe k. Then:

From equation 1: k + a² = 135

From equation 2: k + b² = 263

Therefore, subtracting these gives b² - a² = 128, which is what we had before.

Alternatively, if we subtract equation 1 from equation 2, we get (k + b²) - (k + a²) = 263 - 135 => b² - a² = 128. So same result.

So, so far, we have that relationship between a and b: b² - a² = 128.

Now, we need another equation to relate a and b. Let's recall that m = log₂ a and n = log₂ b. Therefore, mn = (log₂ a)(log₂ b). Wait, but in the equations, we have mn/2. Hmm.

Wait, in the original substitution, we had mn/2 in both equations. So if we let k = mn/2, then the equations become:

k + a² = 135

k + b² = 263

Therefore, we can express k as 135 - a² and also as 263 - b². Therefore, 135 - a² = 263 - b² => b² - a² = 128, which is consistent.

But we need another relation. Let's think about mn. Since m = log₂ a and n = log₂ b, then mn = (log₂ a)(log₂ b). So k = mn/2 = (log₂ a)(log₂ b)/2.

But we can also relate a and b through exponentials. Since m = log₂ a, then a = 2^m. Similarly, b = 2^n.

Therefore, a and b are both powers of 2. That might be helpful. Let's note that:

a = 2^m

b = 2^n

Therefore, a² = (2^m)^2 = 2^{2m}

Similarly, b² = 2^{2n}

So substituting back into the equations:

First equation: (mn)/2 + 2^{2m} = 135

Second equation: (mn)/2 + 2^{2n} = 263

Hmm, but m and n are exponents here. So this might not be straightforward. Alternatively, maybe we can express everything in terms of m and n.

Since a = 2^m and b = 2^n, then ab = 2^{m + n}, which would be the product. The problem states that ab can be written as p√q, so 2^{m + n} = p√q. Therefore, m + n is the exponent, and we need to find integers p and q such that 2^{m + n} = p√q. But since 2^{m + n} is 2^k for some k, unless m + n is a half-integer, but since m and n are real numbers (logarithms), but a and b are positive real numbers.

Wait, but maybe m and n are integers? Because if a and b are powers of 2, then m and n would be integers, but the problem doesn't specify that. Hmm, maybe we can check if assuming m and n are integers leads to a solution.

Alternatively, maybe m and n are rational numbers. Let's see. Let me try to express k in terms of m and n. From the first equation:

k = 135 - a² = 135 - 2^{2m}

Similarly, k = 263 - b² = 263 - 2^{2n}

Therefore, 135 - 2^{2m} = 263 - 2^{2n}

Which simplifies to 2^{2n} - 2^{2m} = 263 - 135 = 128

So 2^{2n} - 2^{2m} = 128

But we also have from earlier, from the difference of squares, that b² - a² = 128. Since a = 2^m and b = 2^n, then (2^n)^2 - (2^m)^2 = 128 => 2^{2n} - 2^{2m} = 128, which is the same equation. So that doesn't give us a new equation.

Therefore, we need another relationship between m and n. Let's recall that mn/2 = k, which is equal to 135 - 2^{2m} and also 263 - 2^{2n}

So maybe we can set up two equations with m and n:

Once the spanning tree edges are built, they form a set of edges that connect all nodes without cycles.

   For the code, perhaps the easiest way is to represent edges as a list of (u, v).

   Initialize a list for tree edges.

   visited = set()

              visited.add(neighbor)

Once adjacency is built:

def generate_initial_part():

    # Step 1: Create spanning tree edges

    # pick a random start node.

    counties = list(adjacency.keys())

    seed = random.choice(counties)

    spanning_tree_edges = []

    # perform BFS to get the edges:

    visited = set([seed])

    q = deque([seed])

    parent = {seed: None} # track parents for edge collection

    while q:

        u = q.popleft()

        adj = adjacency[u]

        for v in adj:

            if v not in visited:

                visited.add(v)

                q.append(v)

                parent[v] = u

    # Now collect edges:

    for v in parent:

        if v != seed:

            u = parent[v]

            spanning_tree_edges.append( (u, v) )

    # Now the edges form the spanning tree.

    # Need to randomly choose 12 edges to cut (break them).

    # Choose 12 edges randomly:

    edge_list = list(spanning_tree_edges)

    cut_edges = random.sample(edge_list, 12)

    # Now, find the connected components of G minus cut_edges:

    # How to compute components:

    # the uncut edges are all other edges in spanning tree.

    forest_edges = [e for e in spanning_tree_edges if e not in cut_edges]

    # build the graph with the forest edges.

    forest_adj = defaultdict(list)

    for u, v in forest_edges:

        forest_adj[u].append(v)

        forest_adj[v].append(u)

    # Now perform BFS to find components.

    components = []

    visited = set()

    for node in counties:

        if node not in visited:

            visited.add(node)

            comp = [node]

            q = deque([node])

            while q:

                current = q.popleft()

                for neighbor in forest_adj[current]:

                    if neighbor not in visited:

                        visited.add(neighbor)

                        comp.append(neighbor)

                        q.append(neighbor)

            components.append(comp)

    # Check if we have exactly 13 districts?

    if len(components)!=13:

        print("Spanning tree problem")

    return [set(comp) for comp in components]

<#sep#>EKS CreateCluster response encryptionConfig property should be a List/Array
# Description

When using moto server, the `CreateCluster` EKS API, the Cluster response object should contain `encryptionConfig` as a List of at-most-one `EncryptionConfig` maps/objects  (as per example here: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/APIReference/API_CreateCluster.html#API_CreateCluster_ResponseSyntax and documented [here](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/APIReference/API_Cluster.html#AmazonEKS-Type-Cluster-encryptionConfig) and [here](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/APIReference/API_EncryptionConfig.html)). Moto returns just a map/object.

The issue causes an error for me when using the terraform AWS provider to create a cluster without encryption_config set - terraform attempts to retry because response marshalling failed, then reports the cluster already exists. The incorrect response is the same no matter which client makes the request though (see aws-cli below), it's a matter of how strictly it matches the documented response and how it behaves when it doesn't match.

### How to reproduce the issue

Start the motoserver v3.1.3 (latest at reporting time) in docker:
```
docker run --rm -d --name moto-bug-test -p 5000:5000 motoserver/moto:3.1.3 
```

Create basic IAM role:
```
aws --region=us-east-1 --endpoint-url=http://localhost:5000 iam create-role \
          --role-name test-cluster \
          --assume-role-policy-document '{"Version": "2012-10-17", "Statement": [{"Action": "sts:AssumeRole", "Effect": "Allow", "Principal": {"Service": "eks.amazonaws.com"}}]}'
```

Run create-cluster with just enough setup and `--debug` to show server responses.
``` 
aws --debug --region=us-east-1 --endpoint-url=http://localhost:5000 eks create-cluster \
          --name test \
          --role-arn $(aws --region=us-east-1  --endpoint-url=http://localhost:5000 iam get-role --role-name test-cluster | jq -r .Role.Arn) \
          --resources-vpc-config "subnetIds=$(aws --region=us-east-1 --endpoint-url=http://localhost:5000 ec2  describe-security-groups |  jq -r '.SecurityGroups[0].GroupId')" 2>&1 | grep -A1 'DEBUG - Response body' | tail -n1 | sed "s/b'//" | sed "s/'//" | jq .cluster.encryptionConfig
```

NOTE: I'm parsing the debug logs here, because the aws client output actually seem to massage the '{}' response from moto to a '[]' before displaying it.

### What I expected to happen

`clusterConfig` should be '[]'

### what actually happens

`clusterConfig` is '{}'

## Algebra Formulas For Class 10

An important algebra formula introduced in class 10 is the quadratic formula. The general form of the quadratic equation is ax2 + bx + c = 0, and there are two methods of solving this quadratic equation. The first method is to solve the quadratic equation by the algebraic method, and the second method is to solve through the use of the quadratic formula. The below formula is helpful to quickly find the values of the variable x with the least number of steps.

In the above expression, the value b2 – 4ac is called the determinant and is useful to find the nature of the roots of the given equation. Based on the value of the determinant, the three types of roots are given below.

• If b2 – 4ac > 0, then the quadratic equation has two distinct real roots.
• If b2 – 4ac = 0, then the quadratic equation has two equal real roots.
• If b2 – 4ac < 0, then the quadratic equation has two imaginary roots.

• And so: y = 2x + 1

## Notation Language And Rigor

Most of the mathematical notation in use today was not invented until the 16th century. Before that, mathematics was written out in words, limiting mathematical discovery.Euler was responsible for many of the notations in use today. Modern notation makes mathematics much easier for the professional, but beginners often find it daunting. According to Barbara Oakley, this can be attributed to the fact that mathematical ideas are both more abstract and more encrypted than those of natural language. Unlike natural language, where people can often equate a word with the physical object it corresponds to, mathematical symbols are abstract, lacking any physical analog. Mathematical symbols are also more highly encrypted than regular words, meaning a single symbol can encode a number of different operations or ideas.

## Inspiration Pure And Applied Mathematics And Aesthetics

Mathematics arises from many different kinds of problems. At first these were found in commerce, land measurement, architecture and later astronomy today, all sciences pose problems studied by mathematicians, and many problems arise within mathematics itself. For example, the physicistRichard Feynman invented the path integral formulation of quantum mechanics using a combination of mathematical reasoning and physical insight, and today’s string theory, a still-developing scientific theory which attempts to unify the four fundamental forces of nature, continues to inspire new mathematics.

Some mathematics is relevant only in the area that inspired it, and is applied to solve further problems in that area. But often mathematics inspired by one area proves useful in many areas, and joins the general stock of mathematical concepts. A distinction is often made between pure mathematics and applied mathematics. However pure mathematics topics often turn out to have applications, e.g. number theory in cryptography.

As in most areas of study, the explosion of knowledge in the scientific age has led to specialization: there are now hundreds of specialized areas in mathematics and the latest Mathematics Subject Classification runs to 46 pages. Several areas of applied mathematics have merged with related traditions outside of mathematics and become disciplines in their own right, including statistics, operations research, and computer science.

In this section, the symbols that are listed are used as some sorts of punctuation marks in mathematical reasoning, or as abbreviations of English phrases. They are generally not used inside a formula. Some were used in classical logic for indicating the logical dependence between sentences written in plain English. Except for the first two, they are normally not used in printed mathematical texts since, for readability, it is generally recommended to have at least one word between two formulas. However, they are still used on a black board for indicating relationships between formulas.

Abbreviation of “because” or “since”. Placed between two assertions, it means that the first one is implied by the second one. For example: “11 is prime it has no positive integer factors other than itself and one.”

These exponential laws are further useful<#sep#>Implement two data structures, `Stack` and `Queue`, using Python classes. Each class should support the following operations:
- `push(item)` / `enqueue(item)`: Add an item to the stack/queue.
- `pop()` / `dequeue()`: Remove and return the top/front item from the stack/queue. Raise an `IndexError` if the operation is performed on an empty structure.
- `peek()`: Return the top/front item without removing it. Raise an `IndexError` if the structure is empty.
- `is_empty()`: Return `True` if the stack/queue is empty, otherwise `False`.
- `size()`: Return the number of items in the stack/queue.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
stack = Stack()
stack.push(1)
stack.push(2)
print(stack.pop())    # Output: 2
print(stack.peek())   # Output: 1
print(stack.is_empty()) # Output: False
print(stack.size())     # Output: 1

queue = Queue()
queue.enqueue(1)
queue.enqueue(2)
print(queue.dequeue()) # Output: 1
print(queue.peek())    # Output: 2
print(queue.is_empty()) # Output: False
print(queue.size())     # Output: 1
```

```python
class Stack:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty list to store stack elements."""
        self.items = []

    def push(self, item):
        """Add an item to the top of the stack."""
        self.items.append(item)

    def pop(self):
        """Remove the item from the top of the stack and return it.
        
        Raises:
            IndexError: If the stack is empty.
        """
        if not self.is_empty():
            return self.items.pop()
        raise IndexError("pop from an empty stack")

    def peek(self):
        """Return the top item of the stack without removing it.
        
        Raises:
            IndexError: If the stack is empty.
        """
        if not self.is_empty():
            return self.items[-1]
        raise IndexError("peek from an empty stack")

    def is_empty(self):
        """Return True if the stack is empty, otherwise False."""
        return len(self.items) == 0

    def size(self):
        """Return the number of items in the stack."""
        return len(self.items)


class Queue:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty list to store queue elements."""
        self.items = []

    def enqueue(self, item):
        """Add an item to the rear of the queue."""
        self.items.append(item)

    def dequeue(self):
        """Remove the item from the front of the queue and return it.
        
        Raises:
            IndexError: If the queue is empty.
        """
        if not self.is_empty():
            return self.items.pop(0)
        raise IndexError("dequeue from an empty queue")

    def peek(self):
        """Return the front item of the queue without removing it.
        
        Raises:
            IndexError: If the queue is empty.
        """
        if not self.is_empty():
            return self.items[0]
        raise IndexError("peek from an empty queue")

    def is_empty(self):
        """Return True if the queue is empty, otherwise False."""
        return len(self.items) == 0

    def size(self):
        """Return the number of items in the queue."""
        return len(self.items)

   Repeat multiple initializations, keep best.

   Record the assignment.

The submission's score will be the sum for all required instances.

Now, considering the scoring function:

d is the number of districts where p* has the most votes (plurality).

For a district, if p* votes > any other party (e.g., Rep votes ≥ Dem votes if it's majority Rep).

Then:

d is count over districts where votes_pstar > 0.5*(total votes in district) (assuming two parties), or the p* has the highest proportion.

The assignment score is d - (b/a).

The code must calculate this.

Computing b and a (max and min district populations):

population of district is the total votes (sum all p’s votes in that county).

So for each district, calculate its total population.

a is min(popu_district), b is max.

Then score is d - b/a.

Therefore, in code:

def compute_score(partition, pstar):

    d_count = 0

    populations = []

    for district in partition.districts:

        total_p = 0

        total_others = 0

        pop_total =0

        for county in district:

            votes = election_data[county]

            pop_total += sum(votes.values())

            # assuming pstar is 'R' or 'D'

            total_p += votes[pstar]

            total_other = sum( [votes[p] for p in votes if p != pstar] )

            # Wait, but pstar is the party. For each county's votes for pstar.

        # need to compute whether pstar's total is greater than any other party's total.

        # Assuming <P> (parties are { 'R', 'D' }, so the other is the other.

        # 其实， The maximum of the other parties (only one other if two parties).

        # so the plurality is if total_p > (total_other).

        if total_p > (pop_total - total_p):

            d_count +=1

        populations.append(pop_total)

    a = min(populations)

    b = max(populations)

    return d_count - ( b / a )

def load_election_data(file_name):

            r = int(row[2])

## Solving General Quadratic Equations By Completing The Square

We can complete the square to solve a Quadratic Equation .

But a general Quadratic Equation can have a coefficient of a in front of x2:

ax2 + bx + c = 0

But that is easy to deal with … just divide the whole equation by “a” first, then carry on:

x2 + x + c/a = 0

Now we can solve a Quadratic Equation in 5 steps:

• Step 1 Divide all terms by a .
• Step 2 Move the number term to the right side of the equation.
• Step 3 Complete the square on the left side of the equation and balance this by adding the same value to the right side of<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that models a book with various attributes and behaviors. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `published_year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
  - `genres`: A list of strings representing the genres of the book.
  - `pages`: An integer representing the number of pages in the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, title, author, published_year, genres=None, pages=None, isbn=None)`: Initializes a new book instance with the given attributes.
  - `describe_book(self)`: Returns a string describing the book, including its title, author, published year, and genres.
  - `add_genre(self, genre)`: Adds a genre to the book's list of genres if it is not already present.
  - `remove_genre(self, genre)`: Removes a genre from the book's list of genres if it exists.
  - `is_classic(self)`: Returns `True` if the book was published more than 100 years ago, otherwise returns `False`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, ["Dystopian", "Political Fiction"])
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Dystopian, Political Fiction"
book.add_genre("Science Fiction")
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Dystopian, Political Fiction, Science Fiction"
book.remove_genre("Dystopian")
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Political Fiction, Science Fiction"
print(book.is_classic())     # Output: True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, published_year, genres=None, pages=None, isbn=None):
        """
        Initializes a new book instance with the given attributes.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param published_year: The year the book was published.
        :param genres: A list of genres of the book.
        :param pages: The number of pages in the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.published_year = published_year
        self.genres = genres if genres is not None else []
        self.pages = pages
        self.isbn = isbn

    def describe_book(self):
        """
        Returns a string describing the book, including its title, author, published year, and genres.
        
        :return: A string describing the book.
        """
        genres_str = ', '.join(self.genres) if self.genres else "None"
        return (f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Published Year: {self.published_year}, "
                f"Genres: {genres_str}")

    def add_genre(self, genre):
        """
        Adds a genre to the book's list of genres if it is not already present.
        
        :param genre: The genre to add.
        """
        if genre not in self.genres:
            self.genres.append(genre)

    def remove_genre(self, genre):
        """
        Removes a genre from the book's list of genres if it exists.
        
        :param genre: The genre to remove.
        """
        if genre in self.genres:
            self.genres.remove(genre)

    def is_classic(self):
        """
        Returns True if the book was published more than 100 years ago, otherwise returns False.
        
        :return: A boolean indicating if the book is a classic.
        """
        current_year = 2023
        return current_year - self.published_year > 100

But in our earlier case, m=3.5, so 2^{3.5}=sqrt(2^7)=sqrt(128)=8√2. That's a valid real number. Similarly for others.

But let's see if there are other pairs. Suppose 2^{2(n - m)} -1 =1, then 2^{2(n - m)} = 2, so 2(n - m)=1, so n - m=0.5. Then 2^{2m} *1 =2^7, so 2m=7 => m=3.5. Then n= m +0.5=4. So that's the solution we found. Alternatively, if 2^{2(n - m)} -1=2^s for some s, then 2^{2m}*2^s=2^7 => 2^{2m +s}=2^7 => 2m +s=7. But s must be an exponent such that 2^{2(n -m)} -1 is a power of two. Let's suppose that 2^{2(n -m)} -1=2^t. Then 2^{2k} -1=2^t, where k= n - m.

Looking for integers t and k such that 2^{2k} -1=2^t. Let's note that 2^{2k} is 4^k. So 4^k -1=2^t.

We need to solve for integers k and t, 4^k -1=2^t.

Looking for integer solutions:

k=1: 4 -1=3=2^1 +1, which is not a power of two.

k=2: 16 -1=15, not a power of two.

k=3: 64 -1=63, not a power of two.

k=0: 1 -1=0=2^{-infty}, not valid.

Negative k? Not really, since k =n -m, and m and n are real numbers but likely positive.

Alternatively, if k is a fraction? Let's see, maybe k=0.5: 4^{0.5} -1=2 -1=1=2^0. So t=0. Then, 4^{0.5} -1=1=2^0. So possible.

So if k=0.5, then 2^{2k}=4^{0.5}=2, so 2 -1=1=2^0.

Therefore, this gives another possible solution. So, if 2^{2(n - m)} -1=1, then t=0, so 2^{2m} *1=2^7 => 2m=7 => m=3.5, and then k= n -m=0.5 => n= m +0.5=4. So same as before. So that gives the same solution.

If k=1.5, 4^{1.5}=8, so 8 -1=7, which is not a power of two. k=0.25: 4^{0.25}=sqrt(2)≈1.414, minus 1≈0.414, not a power of two.

Therefore, the only integer solution is when k=0.5, t=0. So only one solution. Therefore, the unique solution is m=3.5, n=4, leading to a=8√2, b=16, and ab=128√2. Hence p=128, q=2, p+q=130.

Therefore, the answer should be 130.

**Final Answer**

</think>To solve the given problem, we start with the equations:

\[
(\log_2 a)(\log_4 b) + a^2 = 135
\]
\[
(\log_4 a)(\log_2 b) + b^2 = 263
\]

First, we convert the logarithms to base 2. Using the change of base formula, \(\log_4 x = \frac{\log_2 x}{2}\), we rewrite the equations:

\[
(\log_2 a) \left(\frac{\log_2 b}{2}\right) + a^2 = 135
\]
\[
\left(\frac{\log_2 a}{2}\right) (\log_2 b) + b^2 = 263
\]

Let \(m = \log_2 a\) and \(n = \log_2 b\). Substituting these into the equations, we get:

\[
\frac{mn}{2} + a^2 = 135
\]
\[
\frac{mn}{2} + b^2 = 263
\]

Subtracting the first equation from the second:

\[
b^2 - a^2 = 128
\]

This can be factored as \((b - a)(b + a) = 128\). Next, we express \(a\) and \(b\) in terms of \(m\) and \(n\):

\[
a = 2^m \quad \text{and} \quad b = 2^n
\]

Substituting these into the difference of squares equation:

\[
2^{2n} - 2^{2m} = 128
\]

Recognizing that \(128 = 2^7\), we solve for \(m\) and \(n\) such that \(2^{2n} - 2^{2m} = 2^7\). The solution is:

\[
2^{2n} = 256 \quad \text{and} \quad 2^{2m} = 128
\]

This gives:

\[
2n = 8 \quad \Rightarrow \quad n = 4
\]
\[
2m = 7 \quad \Rightarrow \quad m = 3.5
\]

Thus, we have:

\[
a = 2^{3.5} = 2^{7/2} = 8\sqrt{2}
\]
\[
b = 2^4 = 16
\]

The product \(ab\) is:

\[
ab = (8\sqrt{2}) \times 16 = 128\sqrt{2}
\]

Therefore, \(ab\) can be written as \(p\sqrt{q}\) where \(p = 128\) and \(q = 2\). The sum \(p + q\) is:

\[
p + q = 128 + 2 = 130
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{130}

def load_adjacency(file_name):

    adj = defaultdict(list) 

    with open(file_name, 'r') as f:

        reader = csv.reader(f)

        for row in reader:

            c_main = row[0].strip()

            others = row[1:]

            for o in others:

                o_name = o.strip() 

                if o_name != c_main: # ignoring self edges?

                    adj[c_main].append(o_name)

                    adj[o_name].append(c_main)

    # remove duplicates and organized.

    for k in adj:

        adj[k] = list(set(adj[k])) 

    return adj

def remove_county(data, adj, county_name):

    new_data = {k:v for k,v in data.items() if k != county_name}

    new_adj = {}

    for cnty in adj:

        if cnty == county_name:

            continue 

        new_adj_list = []

        for neighbor in adj[cnty]:

            if neighbor != county_name:

                new_adj_list.append(neighbor)

        new_adj[cnty] = new_adj_list 

    for cnty in new_adj: 

        new_adj[cnty] = list(set(new_adj[cnty])) 

    return new_data, new_adj

class Partition:

    def __init__(self, election_data, adj, districts):

        self.election_data = election_data  

        self.districts = districts # a list of sets of county names.

        self.population_cache = []

        self.compute_caches()

    def compute_caches(self):

        self.d_count = 0

        self.populations = []

        self.win_status = []

        for dist in self.districts:

            total_dist_R =0 

            total_dist_D =0 

            pop_total =0 

            for county in dist:

                d_v = self.election_data[county]['D']

                r_v = self.election_data[county]['R']

                pop_total += d_v + r_v

                total_dist_D +=d_v 

                total_dist_R +=r_v 

            if total_dist_R > total_dist_D:

                win = 'R'

            elif total_dist_R < total_dist_D:

                win = 'D'

            else:

                win = 'tied' # but in score, this counts if the pstar is winner, so need to adjust.

                # but the problem says "plurality", so tiebreaker?

            # assuming the problem counts a tie as not having majority (unless a tie is majority, but can't be.)

            # for计算d_count, only when the count is strictly higher:

            self.win_status.append( 'R' if total_dist_R > total_dist_D else 'D')

            self.populations.append(pop_total)

        self.a = min(self.populations)

        self.b = max(self.populations)

        self.d_count = sum(1 for ws in self.win_status if ws == self.pstar)

    def compute_score(self, pstar):

        self.pstar = pstar

        self.d_count = 0 

        for dist in self.districts:

            # recalculate for this pstar 

            # but this is redundant. Perhaps better to precompute for both

            # 但是 may be time-consuming.

            # 或者, recompute here:

            total_p =0 

            total_others =0 

            for county in dist:

                v = self.election_data[county]

                p = v[pstar]

                others = v['D'] + v['R'] - p 

                total_p += p 

                total_others += others 

            if total_p > total_others:

                self.d_count +=1 

        self.a = min( self.populations )

        self.b = max( self.populations )

        return self.d_count - (self.b / self.a )

    def is_valid(self, adj):

        for district in self.districts:

            if not self.is_connected(district, adj):

                return False 

        return True 

    @staticmethod 

    def is_connected(nodes_set, adj):

        start = next(iter(nodes_set))

        visited = set()

        q = deque()

        q.append(start)

        visited.add(start)

        while q:

            current = q.popleft()

            for neighbor in adj[current]:

                if neighbor in nodes_set and neighbor not in visited:

                    visited.add(neighbor)

                    q.append(neighbor)

        return len(visited) == len(nodes_set)

def generate_initial_partition(adj):

    counties = list(adj.keys())

    random.shuffle(counties)

    # perform BFS to build spanning tree.

    seed = random.choice(counties)

    adj_dict 输入是 adj的.

    spanning_tree_edges = build_spanning_tree(adj, seed)

    # choose edges to cut:

    edges_to_cut = random.sample(spanning_tree_edges, 12)

    # 构建 the components:

    forest_edges = [e for e in spanning_tree_edges if e not in edges_to_cut]

    components = find_components( forest_edges, adj )

    return components

To solve the gerrymandering challenge, we need to partition North Carolina's counties into 13 districts optimized for a specified party while ensuring each district is connected based on adjacency data. The solution must maximize the number of districts where the specified party wins (plurality) while minimizing the ratio of the largest to smallest district populations.

### Approach

1. **Data Loading and Parsing**: Read county voting data and adjacency information from CSV files.
2. **Graph Representation**: Construct the adjacency list for the counties to ensure the districts are connected.
3. **Initial Partitioning**: Use a spanning tree of the county graph to divide it into 13 districts. Randomly select edges from the spanning tree to split into the desired number of districts.
4. **Local Search Optimization**: Improve the partition using simulated annealing, where random swaps between counties in neighboring districts are evaluated for better scores and connectivity.

### Solution Code

```python
import sys
import random
import csv
from collections import deque, defaultdict
import math

def load_election_data(file_name):
    counties = {}
    with open(file_name, 'r') as f:
        reader = csv.reader(f)
        headers = next(reader)
        for row in reader:
            name = row[0].strip()
            d_votes = int(row[1])
            r_votes = int(row[2])
            counties[name] = {'D': d_votes, 'R': r_votes}
    return counties

def load_adjacency(file_name):
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    with open(file_name, 'r') as f:
        reader = csv.reader(f)
        for row in reader:
            if not row:
                continue
            main = row[0].strip()
            for neighbor in row[1:]:
                neighbor = neighbor.strip()
                if neighbor != main:
                    adj[main].append(neighbor)
                    adj[neighbor].append(main)
    for k in adj:
        adj[k] = list(set(adj[k]))  # Remove duplicates
    return adj

def remove_county(data, adj, county_name):
    new_data = {k: v for k, v in data.items() if k != county_name}
    new_adj = defaultdict(list)
    for county in adj:
        if county != county_name:
            for neighbor in adj[county]:
                if neighbor != county_name:
                    new_adj[county].append(neighbor)
    for county in new_adj:
        new_adj[county] = list(set(new_adj[county]))  # deduplicate
    return new_data, new_adj

class Partition:
    def __init__(self, election_data, adj, districts):
        self.election_data = election_data
        self.districts = districts
        self.adj = adj
        self.compute_caches()

    def compute_caches(self):
        self.d_win_count = 0
        self.populations = []
        for dist in self.districts:
            r_total = 0
            d_total = 0
            pop = 0
            for county in dist<#sep#>ecs.run_task, ecs_describe_tasks, ecs.list_tags_for_resource not handle tags
